The entries stand, since the convergence between their reading and the source's is independent and predates the source's adoption; each of cf-pr-01 through cf-pr-05 gains a Convergence field recording this; and the entries are not revised in any session where the extraction is loaded. Adds a Candidate Log row deferring admission of non-verse specimens, following the kernel's widening of the specimen at 0.3.17 and the workshop's found-text treatment at 0.7.0. Confinement Bay runs extraction and trials on a found text at runtime, because the bay is the consumer and the knowledge test is a test of position, which any text has. Admission of a non-verse specimen as a catalog entry is deferred, because the recurrence findings rest on a corpus of whole poems and a mixed corpus would change what they measure. The deferral is the operator's call and is recorded in the boot's Outstanding list.

Section D settles the overlay cap: a dial overlay counts against it, and runs as a pair with the same trial without it. Adds two Candidate Log rows. No change to any entry, the selection rule, or the combination table.

A catalog extension for the Portable Poetics Kernel v0.3. It holds confinements extracted from read specimens, in a schema that supports search, reasoned selection, and combination. It runs standalone under the kernel and requires no private core. Its principal consumer is the JIG Workshop Mode's Confinement Bay, which cannot run without it.

A confinement is not a device and not an engine. A device is a local operation. An engine is a whole-poem drive. A confinement is the enclosure inside which an engine runs: the set of limits that decide what the poem is able to know, and therefore what it is able to say. One confinement admits several engines, which is why it sits above Engine Bay in the workshop's scale order.

## Module Manifest

Module name: Confinement Inventory

Module type: diagnostic add-on

Kernel requirement: Portable Poetics Kernel v0.3.14, for Confinement Bay Compatibility

Private-core requirement: none

Optional full-monty enhancement: a full monty plugin may supply source-bearing calibration beneath an entry. Every entry in this file is procedural and runs without one.

Exports:

- the confinement structural model
- the entry schema
- composite and primitive entries with search attributes
- the overlay set
- the combination and cancellation table
- the selection rule
- the breach scan
- rights and leak control for derived structure
- the candidate log
- failure modes

Imports from kernel:

- Engine vs. Residue, for the boundary against engine
- Checkpoint 2, Period Fit
- Checkpoint 6, Decoration vs. Diagnosis
- Checkpoint 9, Weak Signal and Absence, which supplies the extraction test
- Source Policy and Source Exposure Levels
- Reassembly Test
- Default Register
- Designation Policy
- Confinement Bay Compatibility

Imports from extension, optional:

- `11-provenance-grading-extension`, the ladder the stake levels are written on. Entries read without it. Auditing a stake field needs it.
- `13-speaker-dials`, whose settings run as overlays under Section D's cap. Entries read without it.

Source policy: entries describe operations and never carry the phrasing that performs them. Public-domain sources are available at Level 4 and are held at Level 0 or 1 here anyway, because an entry that needs an illustrative line has not finished describing its operation. In-copyright sources are held at Level 0, structure only. The Reassembly Test runs on the composite entry itself, not only on quotations. See Section F.

Failure condition: the file is not standalone. Without the kernel it has no absence test, no period-fit line, and no exposure ceiling. Without a consumer it is a catalog nothing reads.

## Structure

- **Entry Schema.** The eleven fields and the four search attributes.
- **A.** Composites (57). Whole-poem, source-anchored, not combinable.
- **B.** Primitives (137). Single operations, combinable.
- **C.** Overlays (1). Additional rules applicable to any confinement. Speaker dial settings from `13-speaker-dials` are overlays under the same definition and cap, and live in that file.
- **D.** Combination and Cancellation.
- **E.** Selection Rule.
- **F.** Rights and Leak Control.
- **G.** Candidate Log.
- **H.** Failure Modes.

Total: 194 entries and 1 overlay, from 57 specimens.

### Why Confinement and Not Constraint

A constraint is elected. Election implies a choice, a choice implies an intention, and the workshop's Intention Substitution failure mode forbids reading intention. Confinement states the limit as something the page enforces, whether or not anyone chose it, which keeps extraction inside the existing prohibition.

The distinction is operative rather than terminological. A poet who selects a form has taken on a constraint and can lift it. A poet who cannot leave a building, cannot name a sovereign, or cannot be believed has a confinement, and the limit reaches what he can know and not only what he can do. An entry whose knowledge test is empty describes a form. It belongs in the Extended Device Inventory, not here.

The kernel carries the technical sense as a registered homograph at v0.3.14. The ordinary carceral sense stays ordinary.

### The Structural Model

Entries are organized on four levels, borrowed from protein structure and used the way the Compartmentalization Engine uses Shigo's tree-pathology model. The levels may be real on the page. The model is the reader's. Period Fit applies: do not attribute the model to the poet.

The borrowing is worth one sentence of mechanics, because it is what makes the level names legible. In a protein the sequence of amino acids is fixed and flat, the chain folds into a three-dimensional shape, and the shape does the work, so two proteins can share long stretches of sequence, fold differently, and do different things. Function lives at the fold and not at the sequence. That relation is the whole reason the model was taken up.

**Position**, the primary level. The epistemic limit. What the writer cannot know from where the poem puts him. This is the essential level and the one an entry cannot omit.

**Surface**, the secondary level. The local repeating operations that show on the page: the recurring rules, the fixed sequence, the prosodic requirement.

**Fold**, the tertiary level. What position and surface do together: the shape they force the material into once both are running and something still has to be said.

Position and surface are flat. Both are listable, and an entry that has named them has produced a specification rather than a reading. The fold is where function lives. Two confinements can share a position and a surface, fold differently, and do different things, which is why the level cannot be derived from the two above it.

Prompt:

> Given that the poem cannot know this, and must do that, what has to happen to the withheld thing for it to reach the reader at all?

The answer is usually one mechanism rather than a list, and it is usually surprising, because a confinement that produced no surprise was a constraint. Two entries calibrate the field. In `cf-wl-01` every route to plain statement is closed, so the poem's one true sentence has to arrive from outside the poem, engraved by a hand entitled to write it; the engraving is in neither the position nor the surface, and is what happens when you have both and still need to speak. In `cf-sb-01` the poem refuses its own resources one at a time, so that when the worst fact arrives there is no technique left in the room to receive it, and the refusal becomes the carriage.

The fold is also what does not transfer, because it was held in shape by what the confinement cost the poet. That is the subject of the `stake` field and of denaturation below.

A fold field that paraphrases the position, or lists the surface rules again, has not been found. See Failure Modes.

**Assembly**, the quaternary level. What the confinement binds with, what it cancels, and what changes when it is combined.

Three further terms are borrowed and no more, since the kernel's Negative Rule Ledger forbids stacking rare terms.

**Active site.** The small region where the confinement does its work. Everything else is approach. Naming it tells a rewriter where to concentrate, and a rewrite that spends its length away from the active site has run the confinement as decoration.

**Denaturation.** Position and surface transfer cleanly between specimens. The fold does not, because the fold was held in place by what the confinement cost the original poet. Transfer without the cost leaves the sequence intact and the function collapsed. This is the characteristic failure of the whole catalog and it has its own field. Where the poet's cost cannot be established, the entry relies on the cost the page shows the speaker bearing; see the stake convention under Entry Schema.

The level names avoid `L0` through `L3`, which the kernel has already registered for Genopersistation. A confinement has no artifact-surface or reception-management level and must not be read on that ladder.

### Composites and Primitives

A composite is the whole confinement of one poem, source-anchored, and is not combinable. Composites stack badly: two composites in one rewrite over-supply refusal, and when everything is withheld nothing reads as withheld.

A primitive is one operation lifted clear of its source. Primitives combine. Combination happens only at primitive level, under the ceiling in Section D.

Each specimen yields one composite and two to four primitives. A specimen that yields no primitive has been read as a style rather than a confinement.

## Entry Schema

```text
id:               cf-[handle initials]-[NN]
name:             lowercase words, never capitals
handle:           the specimen handle
rights ceiling:   exposure level available, and the level held here
axis:             upward / sideways / inward / outward
imposed by:       the agency that sets the limit
position:         [primary]
surface:          [secondary]
fold:             [tertiary]
active site:      [where the work happens]
forbidden:        [the blocked defaults]
knowledge test:   [what becomes unknowable]
stake:            [the cost the page shows, at P0; any cost to the poet, graded; what does not transfer]
assembly:         [combines with / cancels with]
engines admitted: [which whole-poem drives this enclosure will run]
device cluster:   [Extended Device Inventory categories]
stand-down:       [none, or the flag]
```

Capitals are reserved for designations. A confinement rendered in capitals will be read as a designation and will acquire a persona, which is a failure mode `0c-voice-designations` already names. Confinement names render in lowercase, like device names.

The four search attributes are `name`, `handle`, `axis`, and `imposed by`. A rewrite command may name a confinement directly or select on any of the other three. See the workshop module's Trigger Grammar.

`axis` records where the limit sits relative to the speaker, which is what selection turns on. Upward means withheld from a power that can punish the speaker. Sideways means withheld behind decorum, in front of people who will supply the missing term themselves. Inward means withheld from the poem itself, so that the poem will not say what it knows. Outward, from v0.5, means withheld by no one: the limit lies past the edge of what the speaker's position can reach, as sleep, death, the future, or the sea do, and the speaker would say what lies there if he could know it. The first three are withholdings. The fourth is a blindness, and it was added because three specimens had been filed inward only because selection needed a value.

Stake convention, from v0.3. A `stake` field states the cost the page shows first, at P0, and any cost to the poet after it. Every claim above P0 carries its level from `11-provenance-grading-extension` in the sentence that makes it. A fold that holds on the page's cost is the entry's evidence. A cost to the poet can strengthen a stake and cannot stand in for one, because an unshown biography is a hypothesis under the kernel's Register Is Not Referent. The seed entries predate the convention and were regraded to it at v0.5.

Id collisions, from v0.6. Where a handle's initials collide with an existing id, the id takes the first two letters of the handle's first word. White Lady would collide with Whoso List and takes cf-wh. Numbered titles in a series, from v0.11, take the first letter of the title and a letter for the number, a for I and b for II: Enueg I is cf-ea and Enueg II cf-eb, and Echo's Bones, which would then collide, falls back to the first-two-letters rule as cf-ec.

## A. Composites

### cf-wl-01 whoso list composite

```text
handle:           Whoso List
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 1
axis:             upward
imposed by:       sovereign ownership of the subject
```

**Position.** The poet wants something that belongs to a power able to destroy him. He may name neither the thing nor the power. He has no access to the thing's interior and knows it only as motion away from him.

**Surface.** Address to rivals and successors. An opening announcement that the pursuit is abandoned. Continued pursuit inside the renouncing sentences. One impossible-task figure, which must describe the medium rather than decorate the complaint. A central vehicle borrowed from an existing poem rather than invented, with the prior claim left legible.

**Fold.** Every route to plain statement is closed, so the poem's one true sentence has to arrive from outside it, in a language that is not the poet's, on the surface of the object itself. The poet reports and does not speak. Danger converts into technique, and the technique is the poem.

**Active site.** The inscription. Everything before it is approach.

**Forbidden.** Sincerity about the power. Complaint. Any interior of the wanted object. Resolution, including belief in the renunciation, including by the poet.

**Knowledge test.** Two things are unknowable. What the object wants, since the poem has no access to it. And whether the pursuit is over, since the poem's own sentences contradict its announcement.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the wanted thing is marked as the property of a sovereign, and the marking reaches the reader as an inscription the speaker reports rather than a claim he makes. Wyatt was held in the Tower in May 1536 during the fall of Anne Boleyn, as standard reference accounts report (P2; the primary record was not located this pass). The identification of the poem's subject with Anne Boleyn is the standard critical reading and circulates without an originating source in what was checked, so it stands at P4 here. The fold does not need it, because an owner who can punish is on the page. That not naming was survival, and cost Wyatt the ability to say the thing plainly, is the apparatus's inference from those claims (P4). Transferred to a subject nobody defends, the surface still assembles and the fold collapses, because a deniable inscription protecting nobody reads as ornament. This composite denatures faster than any other in the file.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-wl-02 through cf-wl-04.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface. Load-bearing foreignness. Defamiliarization where the inscribed language is foreign to the poem's register.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position, synecdoche, spatial deixis), D (figural and rhetorical), E (turn, closure), G (self-implication).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sc-01 snow crust composite

```text
handle:           Snow Crust
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 1
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       neighborhood decorum; the register the speaker has available
```

**Position.** The speaker is settling a public question about private conduct, in front of neighbors, for the record. He is entirely in earnest. He does not know that his subject sits below the register he is using.

**Surface.** Ballad quatrains, tetrameter and trimeter alternating. Internal rhyme at the caesura of every long line; end rhyme on the short lines. Mechanically checkable, so check it. A fixed argument order: scriptural precedent, the question posed as two options, argument from nature, argument from thrift, named witnesses for and against, verdict as proverb.

**Fold.** The register holds the speaker's sincerity in place while the argument does damage he has not authorized. Because he cannot see the gap, the poem's second-order finding arrives without a satirist to credit it. The disclosure of the local case then runs on decorum rather than on evidence: courtesy supplies everything except the name, and the reader convicts.

**Active site.** The withheld case and the interval that completes it.

**Forbidden.** Irony. Any signal that the speaker knows his register exceeds his subject. Metaphor beyond the proverbial. Any word a person arguing in a tavern would not use. Drawing the conclusion.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the register gap, which is what forbids irony and removes the entire comic output space. He also cannot assert the accusation, only the facts that compose it.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the speaker declines to name one woman, says he fears to offend her, and places her in the town and within the past year. The anthology that prints the poem in this session's corpus calls it a New England broadside and dates it about 1786 (P2 as that anthology's claim; the anthology's identity is P4). That the woman existed, that the poem was read by people who knew her, and that the arithmetic was local knowledge are unshown and stand at P4, and Register Is Not Referent keeps the real and invented readings both live. The fold holds on the page's cost either way, since the speaker's stated fear of offense is what the withholding answers. Transferred, the refusal to name costs nothing and the arithmetic becomes a set problem, which is disclosure pretending to be withholding.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-sc-02 through cf-sc-05.

**Engines admitted.** Floor-origin hypothesis. Accidental ars poetica. Plain speech as anti-laundering.

**Device cluster.** A (exemplum, temporal deixis, distance-marking demonstratives), B (internal rhyme, ballad measure), E (fixed argument sequence, proverbial closure), G (pragmatic and ethical).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sb-01 small branch composite

```text
handle:           Small Branch
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             inward
imposed by:       a fixed vantage; the destruction of the site and its record
```

**Position.** One stationary vantage, engine running. The world passes and the speaker does not. Everything must be knowable from this spot or from having been a child on this spot.

**Surface.** Justified prose block, no line breaks, prosody suppressed so that nothing can be beautiful by lineation. The site described by three nested subtractions: the thing removed, the record of the thing removed with it, the private memorial removed last. The speaker's own childhood placed on the identical coordinates, parenthetical and subordinate.

**Fold.** The poem refuses its own resources one at a time, so that when the worst fact arrives there is no technique available to receive it. The fact enters once, flat, and the poem returns to the removals without looking back. A destroyed amateur memorial had been doing the work of holding the event in place; the poem assumes that office and never says so.

**Active site.** The flat predicate, and the position of the private memorial as the last thing removed.

**Forbidden.** First person after the vantage is established. Statements of feeling. Witnesses who understand what they are seeing. Consolation of any kind, including the consolation of announcing that consolation is refused. Adjectives and adverbs inside the fact.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot approach, cannot ask, and cannot confirm. He knows the site only as a former place. He cannot know what the passing faces see, and the poem must not supply it.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the poem reports a murdered child and does not raise its voice, and the flatness is pressure management rather than technique. Whether the reported crime occurred is unshown in this session, which is the reason the kernel's Register Is Not Referent was calibrated on this specimen, and the entry needs only what the page reports. Applied to a low-stakes source the surface assembles and produces wryness, because there is nothing to manage. The clearest denaturation case in the file and the reason the field exists.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-sb-02 through cf-sb-04.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default. Transparent ugliness. Compartmentalization, where the block walls the fact off rather than resolving it.

**Device cluster.** A (witness posture, reportorial stance, macro-to-micro compression, scene and report alternation), C (compound predicate, passive construction), F (justified block, prosody suppression), G (appropriation risk, voyeurism risk, sayability).

**Stand-down.** None. Rights-limited to Level 0 in this file, which is a separate limit.

### cf-hu-01 huswifery composite

```text
handle:           Huswifery
rights ceiling:   completed about 1685, first published 1939; rights uncertain, held at Level 0
axis:             upward
imposed by:       an addressee who alone can make the speaker what he asks to be
```

**Position.** The speaker wants a condition he may not claim and cannot confirm, because the only party able to confer it is the one he is addressing. He knows what he would be if the request were granted. He does not know whether it has been.

**Surface.** Petition throughout: every main verb is a request addressed upward. One governing conceit, a manufacturing sequence taken in the order the trade requires, from raw material to finished garment. The speaker supplies his own faculties as the equipment at each stage and is never the operator. Six-line stanzas, a quatrain and a couplet, in pentameter; mechanically checkable, so check it. The only claim about the speaker's future is a result clause at the close.

**Fold.** The speaker's standing can be neither asserted nor asked about directly, so it is routed through a process the reader can check stage by stage. Each request is small and plausible, and the sequence carries the reader to the one stage no part can perform, where the making is handed wholly to the addressee. The standing then surfaces as appearance rather than knowledge: what the speaker cannot know about himself, observers will be able to see on him, if everything asked for is done.

**Active site.** The stage where the speaker stops supplying parts and asks the addressee to do the making. Everything before it is inventory.

**Forbidden.** Any indicative claim about the speaker's own state. The speaker as agent of any stage. Thanks, which would presume the petition granted. Doubt stated as doubt, which would turn petition into complaint. A stage out of the trade's order.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether he is what he asks to be. He knows the process and its order and not its outcome, which belongs to another.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), assertion is closed to the speaker and every line spends its grammar on request. The poem was completed about 1685 and first published in 1939 in Thomas H. Johnson's edition of the poetical works (P1). Accounts that Taylor told his heirs not to publish are widespread, and no primary record was located in what was checked, so the claim stands at P4 here; if it holds, the closing display had no reader in the poet's lifetime, which sharpens the condition without being needed for the fold. The reading that a claim of assurance would presume on the addressee is the apparatus's account of the frame (P4). Transferred to a petition aimed at a power that cannot withhold, the requests read as instructions to a contractor and the closing display as self-advertisement.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-hu-02 through cf-hu-04.

**Engines admitted.** Floor-origin hypothesis, where household work supplies the force the poem credits to its addressee. Defamiliarization, where the trade sequence makes doctrine strange enough to be seen.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, first/second-person management), C (imperative, parallelism, list syntax), D (conceit), E (stanza system, couplet).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-et-01 eros turannos composite

```text
handle:           Eros Turannos
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       the walls of a private marriage; a town that watches it from outside
```

**Position.** The poem's knowledge of a marriage is the neighbors' conjecture. It cannot know what either partner sees, fears, or intends, and for most of its length it does not say so.

**Surface.** Octaves with a fixed rhyme scheme, a feminine rhyme carried across both halves of each stanza, and a triplet in the second half; mechanically checkable, so check it. The first two-thirds narrate both interiors with the confidence of full access. A late stanza discloses the teller as plural and local and turns to address the reader. Neither partner speaks.

**Fold.** The poem spends its access before it discloses its position. When the teller turns out to be the town, every interior the reader has accepted becomes the town's story, and the reader, who accepted it, is shown to have been listening with the town. The one thing the poem can then say with authority is that nobody outside a marriage can narrate it, and the reader's earlier belief is the evidence.

**Active site.** The stanza where the teller becomes plural and concedes the telling is guesswork. Everything before it is loaded for the retraction.

**Forbidden.** Direct speech from either partner. Confirmation from inside the house. Placing the disclosure first. Guesses too implausible to be believed, which would leave the retraction nothing to retract. A single named neighbor in place of the town.

**Knowledge test.** The poem cannot know either interior and cannot know whether its telling reaches the house. It knows what shows from outside, over years.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the town's: it has watched and talked and cannot enter, and its closing claim of harmlessness is its only defense. Emma Robinson, the poet's sister-in-law, read the poem as a record of her marriage to his brother Herman and glossed it as despotic love; her reading is reported in a 2007 Contemporary Poetry Review essay, Aspects of Robinson (P2, at second hand). That the poet stood where the poem's town stands is the apparatus's inference (P4), and the entry does not rest on it: the fold needs the town's cost, and the town is on the page. What does not transfer is the reader's prior belief. On low-stakes material the disclosure reads as a trick ending and the retraction retracts a joke.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-et-02 through cf-et-04.

**Engines admitted.** Access-asymmetry, where the biographical reading opens a second poem under the town's. Anti-laundering engine default, where the closing claim of harmlessness launders what the telling has done.

**Device cluster.** A (first/second/third-person management, pronoun shift, addressee position), E (ballad architecture, stanza system, turn), G (implication, plausible deniability, reader activation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ap-01 apple-picking composite

```text
handle:           Apple-Picking
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             outward
imposed by:       exhaustion at the edge of a sleep the speaker cannot classify; the absence of the one witness who could
```

**Position.** The speaker has stopped work and is going under. He cannot know what kind of sleep it is, and the one creature whose experience would settle it is gone.

**Surface.** Lines of irregular length, from a single foot to six; rhyme almost continuous and in no fixed scheme; mechanically checkable, so check it. Present tense, one evening. An inventory of what was left undone opens the poem. The finished work returns as sensation after it has been declared over. The close refers the question to the absent creature and leaves it there.

**Fold.** Because the speaker cannot know what the sleep is, he can only report its onset, and the onset arrives made of the day's work. The reader learns what is coming from what it contains, and what it contains is labor repeating itself in the senses, which is the one reading of the sleep the speaker can support. The graver reading is never stated. It becomes available only through the referral, since a question that an animal's hibernation could settle is already a question about more than rest.

**Active site.** The admission that the speaker does not know which sleep it is, and the referral that follows it.

**Forbidden.** Naming death. Rest as reward. Consolation for the unfinished remainder. Deciding the sleep's kind. Figurative labor: the work stays literal throughout, or the undecidability collapses into allegory.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether the sleep is ordinary or something more, and cannot ask the one witness whose sleep is the comparison. He knows the day, its residue, and its waste.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the body is exhausted and still working, and the undecidability rests on that fatigue being literal rather than figured. The poem was collected in North of Boston in 1914 (P4 here: from the apparatus's knowledge, not checked this pass). The fold denatures where the labor is decoration. A speaker who has done no work cannot have it return in sleep, and the open question becomes a wink at mortality.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ap-02 through cf-ap-04. The specimen also runs a variant of cf-wl-04, disbelieved renunciation: the speaker announces the work is over and the work continues in the sleep rather than in the syntax. See the recorded recurrence in Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal, for the graver reading carried at low amplitude. Defamiliarization, where the estranged sight established early governs what the sleep shows.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position, tense shift), B (end rhyme, rhyme without scheme), E (open ending), F (line length policy), G (withholding, embodied attention quality).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-he-01 hellenistics composite

```text
handle:           Hellenistics
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       a historical sequence the speaker claims to see whole; a future he cannot reach
```

**Position.** The speaker can see, as he understands it, the whole course of a civilization, including the collapse he expects and what follows it. He cannot see or reach the one thing that would matter to the people who live after it, and he addresses them knowing they will not have the poem.

**Surface.** Long lines in three numbered movements. The first moves through objects and figures from a childhood and a classical past, and revokes the credit it has just given. The second states contempt for humankind in its untaught state and praises one culture for having reduced a short list of vices. The third predicts decline in the present age, turns to second-person address to descendants, and ends in questions about who will teach or warn them.

**Fold.** The prophecy's knowledge is total everywhere except at the point that decides whether the future is worth having, so the poem has to stop there, and it stops as questions put to people who cannot hear them. The questions ask for a poet and a prophet. The poem has just been both, and the asking concedes that it will not be there, so its authority is spent establishing a future in which it is absent.

**Active site.** The closing questions, and the one that asks for a poet.

**Forbidden.** Grief for the civilization, which the poem tells its addressees to withhold. Consolation for the present. An answer to the closing questions. Any sign that the address will arrive. Softening the contempt or the violence the last question licenses.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether any teacher or warner will be born, and cannot reach the people the poem addresses. He knows the pattern and not its one variable.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the poem's authority rests on its certainty about history, and that certainty makes its single ignorance absolute: a prophet who knew the answer would have to give it. The poem appeared in Such Counsels You Gave to Me (1937), among poems a descriptive bibliography of Jeffers dates 1935 to 1938 (P1). The contempt and the closing license to violence are on the page (P0) and default to engine under the kernel's anti-laundering rule, so an extraction that keeps the unreachable-address fold and drops them has laundered the specimen. Transferred to a speaker whose foresight is partial, the closing questions lose their edge and read as uplift; the denaturation runs toward hope.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-he-02 and cf-he-03.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default. Transparent ugliness.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, temporal deixis, micro-to-macro scale shift), C (interrogation, correctio), F (line length policy, numbering), G (reader activation).

**Stand-down.** None. A full monty plugin for this specimen exists and was not loaded; this entry was extracted from the corpus text.

### cf-wa-01 wife's affection composite

```text
handle:           Wife's Affection
rights ceiling:   first publication not checked; held at Level 0
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a bond with no public standing; a secret the speaker keeps for another
```

**Position.** The speaker holds a standing the world does not acknowledge and may not claim it. She knows what she is; nobody around her can, and she cannot tell them.

**Surface.** Twenty lines of trochaic tetrameter, printed in this corpus as one block in a normalized text, so check meter and stanza form against an edition and read nothing from punctuation. An opening question that refuses interference. A catalog of endurances in paired noun phrases. A day divided into a public display and a private one. A closing secret, contained, its release scheduled for after death, addressed to a second person the poem does not name.

**Fold.** Standing that can be neither claimed in public nor witnessed in private has to be proved by what it costs, so the poem inventories the cost, and the inventory is the only public face the secret has. The one party who could confirm the standing appears only at the end, as the destination of a disclosure postponed past death, which puts confirmation out of the poem's reach by construction.

**Active site.** The statement that no one suspects her standing. It turns the inventory before it from complaint into evidence.

**Forbidden.** Naming the bond. Naming the addressee. Disclosing the secret or hinting at its content. Complaint without the counter-claim. A witness who knows.

**Knowledge test.** No one in the poem's world can know her standing, the reader cannot know the secret, and the poem cannot confirm that the addressee knows either.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: public endurance with no public recognition, borne by a body the poem inventories. Biographical identifications of the addressee circulate in the criticism, and none is checked or relied on here (P4 as a class). The fold holds because the secret carries the speaker's standing. Transferred to a secret that costs its keeper nothing, the containment reads as coyness and the inventory as complaint.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-wa-02 through cf-wa-04. The composite runs a claim of standing and a refusal to disclose together; only cf-wa-04 is a refusal under the Section D ceiling.

**Engines admitted.** Compartmentalization, where the secret is sealed off to keep the rest of the life standing. Body-cost rule, since the inventory is carried by a body.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, speaker position), B (meter), C (interrogation, list syntax), D (antithesis), G (withholding, credibility management).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-cl-01 cannot live composite

```text
handle:           Cannot Live
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             upward
imposed by:       an order above the pair that closes life, death, and resurrection alike
```

**Position.** The speaker has tried every form of union with the addressee, in this life, in dying, and after death, and finds each closed by an order neither of them controls. She cannot know how that order would judge them.

**Surface.** Short lines, mostly trimeter and dimeter, in quatrains with loose rhyme on the even lines and a longer closing stanza. The corpus prints the 1890 editors' regularized text as one block, so check meter, stanza breaks, and punctuation against a modern edition. An argument by elimination: each mode of union is proposed, ruled out, and given its reason, in the order a life runs. A conclusion drawn from the eliminations. A closing inventory of what remains between the two.

**Fold.** Because every channel is closed by the same order, separation arrives as a deduction rather than a choice, and then the separation becomes the only channel left. The closing inventory names what stays open, and everything on it is something distance supplies. The argument that proves they cannot meet ends by describing the only way they still do.

**Active site.** The conclusion and the inventory that follows it, where the closure is renamed as an opening.

**Forbidden.** An open alternative. A reason that is circumstantial rather than ordained. Blame of the addressee. Hope that outlasts the inventory. Consolation from the order that forbids them.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know how they would be judged, and the poem asks it as a real question in the middle of the argument. She knows the reason against each union and not the verdict.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the argument costs the speaker every consolation her order offers, the afterlife included, and one of her reasons concedes that the addressee outshines the divine, which is the fault the judgment she cannot foresee would weigh. The poem was first published in 1890 in Poems, under the editors' title In Vain (P1). Biographical identifications of the addressee circulate, and none is checked or relied on here (P4 as a class). Transferred to obstacles that are circumstantial, a job or a distance, the elimination reads as melodrama and the closing inventory as a sulk.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-cl-02 through cf-cl-04.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent argument as a phase tension, where the logic carries the grief.

**Device cluster.** C (conditionals, subordination, interrogation, list syntax), A (addressee position), B (meter, slant rhyme), E (closure, turn).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-mc-01 many are called composite

```text
handle:           Many Are Called
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             upward
imposed by:       a sovereign who admits by a law no one has read
```

**Position.** The speaker stands, with everyone else, outside a sealed domain whose ruler admits whom he will by a law no one has read. He cannot know whether he will be admitted, and the only admissions he can report have fallen on the dead.

**Surface.** A Petrarchan sonnet, octave and sestet; mechanically checkable, so check it. The octave establishes the sealed domain and the failure of every appeal to it. The sestet reports the rare admissions: their intervals unpredictable, their cause unseen, their rule unread, their recipients already dead. Third person throughout. The speaker never places himself.

**Fold.** Because the rule of selection cannot be read, the poem can only say where the admissions have been seen to land, and they land on the dead. Recognition is posthumous on the only evidence available, which places the living speaker among the appeals that fail, and the poem never says so. The reader does the counting the speaker omits.

**Active site.** The close, where the admission falls on the dead. In this corpus text the line's adjective is lost to OCR; the site's structure survives.

**Forbidden.** The speaker's first person. A named poet, chosen or refused. Complaint about neglect. Any reading of the law. Hope that the living may be admitted.

**Knowledge test.** No one can know the law of selection or its intervals. The speaker cannot know whether he is chosen, and his evidence reaches only those who can no longer know it.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the speaker's cost is his own standing, which the sonnet cannot claim, since the sonnet is itself an appeal of the kind its octave says fails. The collection, The Three Taverns (1920), is P4 here, not checked this pass. That the sonnet draws on Robinson's own long wait for recognition is the apparatus's inference (P4), and the entry does not rest on it. Transferred to a speaker who counts himself among the neglected, the sonnet becomes complaint; transferred to one with no stake in being chosen, it becomes a lecture on genius.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-mc-02 through cf-mc-04.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal, for the speaker's own placement carried at low amplitude.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position, third-person management), B (meter), E (sonnet architecture, turn), G (implication, reader activation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-pr-01 prufrock composite

```text
handle:           Prufrock
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a room in which the question has no admissible form; a hearer the speaker trusts not to repeat him
```

**Position.** The speaker has a question he cannot ask in the room where it would have to be asked, and cannot state even to the reader. He can know its reception only by imagining it refused.

**Surface.** A dramatic monologue in rhymed verse paragraphs of irregular length, lines from two stresses to seven, rhyme recurring without a scheme; check it. An epigraph in a foreign language that sets the terms of the speech. Addressed to an unspecified second person. Repeated self-questioning about whether he has the nerve or the right. A middle section that turns to the conditional perfect and imagines the occasion as already past. The question is referred to and never stated. The close moves to the first person plural and ends in a collective waking that is also a death.

**Fold.** Because the question cannot be asked, the poem conducts the asking in retrospect: the occasion is gone before the poem reaches it, and the only reply the question ever receives is the correction the speaker writes for the listener. The epigraph supplies the terms under which the monologue can exist at all, confession to a hearer who will not carry it back, so the reader is cast as that hearer and receives what the room never will.

**Active site.** The turn into the conditional perfect, and the imagined correction it produces.

**Forbidden.** The question in words. A reply from anyone but the speaker's imagination. An asking in the present tense. An identified listener. Self-knowledge that would resolve the hesitation.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know what the question is in words and cannot know how it would be answered. The reader cannot know the question either.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: he pays for the unasked question in years spent on the room's small rituals, and the monologue is the only place the question is spent. First publication in Poetry in 1915 is P4 here, not checked this pass. The confinement's portability is its hazard. Its surface, hesitation and self-mockery and the unstated question, is the most imitated manner of any specimen in the file (P4, the apparatus's judgment), and transferred to a speaker whose question is trivial or askable, the hesitation reads as mannered indecision with none of the cost.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-pr-02 through cf-pr-05.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal, for the question carried by everything except its statement. Access-asymmetry, where the untranslated epigraph gives one reader the premise and withholds it from another.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, first/second-person management, pronoun shift), B (refrain, end rhyme), C (interrogation, conditionals), E (dramatic monologue, open ending), G (self-implication, withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

**Convergence.** `14-attridge-staten-extraction` reprints and reads this specimen. The entry was extracted at catalog v0.4, before that extension existed, and stands under Section F's prior-entry clause: the convergence with the source's reading is independent, the entry is not revised in any session where the extension is loaded, and no further entries are extracted from the specimen.

### cf-ph-01 philomela composite

```text
handle:           Philomela
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       belatedness; a tradition that admits the speaker only as a visitor
```

**Position.** The speaker is heir to a literary tradition whose central song never reached his country. When he goes to hear it where it lives, he cannot hear what the tradition heard, and he cannot tell whether the fault is the song's or his.

**Surface.** Five-line stanzas rhymed abbaa, the rhymes comic and polysyllabic, the lines uneven in length; check it. Latinate, mock-pedantic diction. An itinerary of the song's past arrivals in other cultures and its non-arrival here, a first-person anecdote of travel to the source and a disappointing hearing, a closing apostrophe that concedes unworthiness.

**Fold.** The poem cannot produce the classical verse it describes, so it says so at the outset and then performs the incapacity: its rhymes and diction are the local verse the first stanza disowns, and the failed hearing at the source confirms from the other side that travel cannot close the gap. The reader hears the inadequacy the speaker reports in the verse that reports it, so the complaint is proved by its form. At the moment of hearing, the poem declines to decide between the bird and the ear.

**Active site.** The failed hearing, where the fault is left unassigned.

**Forbidden.** A successful hearing. Assigning the failure. Verse that sounds like the tradition it disowns. Sincere lament: the register stays comic, or the complaint turns into self-pity.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether the song or his hearing is at fault. He knows the tradition's reports and his own disappointment, and nothing that reconciles them.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's standing as a poet in the tradition he is using, and his own verse is the evidence against him. The collection, Chills and Fever (1924), is P4 here, not checked this pass. The page places the speaker at Oxford (P0 as the poem's claim). That the poet heard a nightingale there is not established and is not needed. Transferred to a speaker with no tradition whose authority he needs, belatedness reads as snobbery or as self-deprecation, and the comic rhymes become light verse.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ph-02 through cf-ph-04.

**Engines admitted.** Load-bearing foreignness, where the tradition's names and diction carry the distance. Access-asymmetry, between readers who know the classical sources and readers who do not.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position, apostrophe), B (end rhyme, comic rhyme), D (allusion), E (stanza system, closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-mt-01 melville's tomb composite

```text
handle:           Melville's Tomb
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             outward
imposed by:       death at sea; the limit of instruments and of elegy
```

**Position.** The elegist stands where his subject stood, at the shore, receiving messages from the drowned that arrive already obscured. He may not claim to read them, no instrument extends his reach, and elegy cannot wake the dead.

**Surface.** Four quatrains in pentameter, with rhyme that binds some lines and not others; check it. Compressed metaphor in which each image is a message-bearing object that arrives damaged. The subject in the third person; the elegist never uses the first. The last stanza names the instruments at the point where they fail and closes on a declaration that one custodian holds what the poem cannot.

**Fold.** Because the poem cannot read what the drowned send, it transmits its own content in the same condition: compressed, partial, legible as message and not as statement. The reader receives the elegy the way the watcher on the shore received the sea's messages, and the poem's difficulty becomes its report of the limit. The close assigns the knowledge to the one party that holds it and cannot give it up.

**Active site.** The last stanza, where the instruments stop and the custodian is named.

**Forbidden.** A decoded message. The elegist's grief stated. A first person. A resurrection. Syntax loosened until the images read as statement.

**Knowledge test.** The watcher cannot read what the drowned send, and no instrument or elegy reaches farther. The knowledge exists and is held elsewhere.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the elegist's: the reading he cannot do. The poem's compression cost it readers, and that is on record. Harriet Monroe, editing Poetry, questioned its metaphors as illogical in a 1926 exchange of letters with Crane, and his reply defending them is a dated document (P1). An earlier offer from Marianne Moore at The Dial to print it without the last stanza, which Crane refused by withdrawing the poem, is reported in a 2007 Contemporary Poetry Review essay (P2, at second hand). The stanza that editor would have cut is the active site. Transferred to material without a real limit, compression is obscurity, and the Monroe objection becomes correct.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-mt-02 through cf-mt-04.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal. Defamiliarization engine, where the compression estranges each object before it can be read.

**Device cluster.** D (compressed metaphor, personification), A (third-person management, speaker position), B (meter, partial rhyme), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-jc-01 jewish cemetery composite

```text
handle:           Jewish Cemetery
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a visitor's position among another people's dead; a house of worship shut against him
```

**Position.** The speaker is a sympathetic visitor among the graves of a community he does not belong to. He can read its dead, its names, and its shut building, and he cannot know its living. He does not know that his closing verdict on the community's future exceeds what that vantage can support.

**Surface.** Quatrains in pentameter, rhymed abab; check it. A visitor's inventory of the site: graves beside a street, stones, names in a foreign accent, a quoted mourner's blessing, a closed synagogue. A turn to questions about how the dead came there. A summary history of their persecution and endurance, taken from outside. A closing general statement about the fate of the people as a whole.

**Fold.** Because the speaker can see only what the community left behind, his sympathy has nowhere to go but into elegy, and the elegy turns a shut building into a finished people. The page carries its own counter-evidence: midway, the speaker notes that someone unseen keeps the graves tended, and he does not ask who. A reader who asks finds the living the verdict has written off. The confinement is visible from the page itself, and the fold works by letting the reader see past the speaker with evidence the speaker supplied.

**Active site.** The closing verdict, read against the unattributed care recorded midway.

**Forbidden.** Irony at the speaker's expense: the sympathy is sincere and the limit is unknowing. A living member of the community. The speaker's awareness of the gap. A correction inside the poem.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the community's living life, and he cannot see that his verdict exceeds his vantage. The reader can see it from the page, since the page records care the verdict cannot account for.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost falls on the subject rather than the speaker: the verdict writes a people off, and the speaker pays nothing he can see. The site's upkeep was funded by bequests from the Touro family, and the synagogue building was preserved until services resumed in 1883, as a National Park Service history reports (P2). That the poem's unseen hand refers to that upkeep is the apparatus's inference (P4), and the page-internal reading does not need it. Emma Lazarus answered the poem with In the Jewish Synagogue at Newport, likely written in 1867 and published in 1871, as a published Theatre Dybbuk podcast transcript reports (P2). Transferred to a draft whose closing verdict is merely mistaken, the confinement is gone. It needs a verdict the speaker's position makes unavoidable and the page makes answerable.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-jc-02 through cf-jc-04. The composite is the catalog's second unknowing-register case, after cf-sc-05, and the first whose gap the page itself can show.

**Engines admitted.** Access-asymmetry, between readers who know the community's later history and readers who do not. Anti-laundering engine default, where the sympathy launders the verdict.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position, spatial deixis, witness posture), B (meter, end rhyme), C (interrogation), E (stanza system, closure), G (credibility management, appropriation risk).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sm-01 smithville composite

```text
handle:           Smithville
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             inward
imposed by:       a child's need for stories; a disbelief that has no story to tell
```

**Position.** A parent knows what he does not believe and cannot pass it to his child, because the only form she can receive is a story and what he knows has none. He can stop the teaching or join it. He cannot counter it.

**Surface.** First person plural, narrowing to the singular, narrating one week in order. Questions the parents put to themselves and do not put to the child. A general statement at the midpoint about what a child can be taught. A children's performance watched from the pews. A drive home. Line breaks not read; see Provenance.

**Fold.** Because disbelief has no story, the speaker's knowledge cannot enter the child's world as a rival account. It can enter only as silence, and the poem makes the silence participatory: the parent ends inside the rite he disbelieves, keeping time with his voice withheld. The reader measures what he knows by how completely he keeps it out of the car.

**Active site.** The close, where the speaker joins the song without sound.

**Forbidden.** Telling the child. Ridicule of the church or its teachers. A counter-story. A verdict on whether the silence was right. Nostalgia for belief stated outright.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot give his child what he knows, and cannot know what the story he disbelieves will make of her. He also cannot tell whether the scene is comic or serious, and he says so.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the parent's: he watches his child take in a story he has left, and his only answer is none. Any biographical referent for the family is unestablished here (P4) and not relied on. Transferred to a speaker whose disbelief could be stated at no cost, or to a child old enough to argue with, the silence reads as cowardice or indifference, and the fold is gone.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-sm-02 and cf-sm-03. The specimen also runs a variant of cf-ap-04, unclassified state: the speaker says he cannot tell the comic from the serious and does not decide. See the recorded recurrences in Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent argument as a phase tension, where the midpoint statement argues its case in the open. Weak signal, for the grief under the comedy.

**Device cluster.** A (first/second/third-person management, pronoun shift), C (interrogation, declarative flatness), E (closure), G (withholding, self-implication).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-hl-01 how to like it composite

```text
handle:           How to Like It
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             inward
imposed by:       a want the man cannot name; an animal voice that names only small ones
```

**Position.** A man cannot know what he wants. The only articulate desires in the poem belong to his dog, whose wants are immediate and small, and the answers the man needs are held in no place he can reach.

**Surface.** Third person, present tense, one evening. Alternation between the man's interior, rendered at length, and the dog's proposals, each a short refrain in the same invitational form. The man's longing becomes an imagined drive, given in sensory detail and never begun. A direct question about wanting everything and nothing. A close in which his wife finds him looking into a household appliance as though answers were stored there. Line breaks not read; see Provenance.

**Fold.** The dog's proposals voice appetites the man cannot own, and each one he lets pass narrows his longing toward something he still cannot name. The poem answers his question only with the dog's last and smallest proposal, which he accepts: the want too large to know is met by the want small enough to do, and the answers are assigned to a kitchen. Comedy is the only register in which this despair can be said at all.

**Active site.** The question about wanting everything and nothing, and the dog's proposal that follows it.

**Forbidden.** The man stating what he wants. The drive taken. The dog made wise. Despair stated without the dog's interruption.

**Knowledge test.** The man cannot know what he wants or how to live with not knowing, and the poem knows no more than he does.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the man's: a longing carried at length and answered by a dog's appetites. Transferred to a speaker whose want could be named, the dog becomes a gag. Transferred to a speaker in open crisis without the comedy's cover, the dog becomes an insult.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-hl-02 and cf-hl-03. The close runs a variant of cf-mt-04, sole custodian, made domestic: the answers are assigned to a place that holds them and cannot give them up.

**Engines admitted.** Complaint loop, where the man's dissatisfaction repeats against each proposal. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (third-person management), B (refrain), C (interrogation, cumulative sentence), D (prosopopoeia), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-me-01 memory composite

```text
handle:           Memory
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             inward
imposed by:       memory that joins things without consent; an answer the poem holds and will not give
```

**Position.** The speaker cannot choose what his memory joins. The poem moves from his own comic linkages to a stranger's, a man wrongly imprisoned for whom every small thing recalls prison, and stops at the moment that man is about to name the memory that owns him.

**Surface.** First person, conversational, digressive. A comic pairing of two childhood dislikes. A general statement about how memories link, with anecdotes of other people's links. A turn to a newspaper interview with a wrongly imprisoned man, reported partly in his quoted speech. A last line in which he says the answer is easy, after which nothing follows. Line breaks not read; see Provenance.

**Fold.** The poem establishes the law of involuntary memory on harmless material, so the reader has accepted the law before it is applied to a man for whom it is a second sentence. Stopping before his answer makes the reader supply it, and whatever the reader supplies, the law the poem has just established says it will not leave him.

**Active site.** The last line, and the absence after it.

**Forbidden.** The man's answer. Comment from the speaker after the interview. A return to the comic material. Pity stated.

**Knowledge test.** The reader cannot know what the man recalled most. The speaker has it from the article and withholds it. The man cannot choose what he remembers, and neither can the speaker.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost moves from the speaker to a stranger: the speaker's links cost him embarrassment, and the man's cost him the freedom he was given back. The article is on the page as the speaker's claim (P0 as claim); whether it was a real article is unshown here (P4), and Register Is Not Referent keeps both readings live. The man's quoted speech includes a racial slur in his own voice; any use of this entry keeps the specimen at Level 0 and does not reproduce it. Transferred to material where the withheld answer costs no one, the stop is a punchline.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-me-02 through cf-me-04.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule, since the cost ends in the man's body and history. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (scene/report alternation, first/second/third-person management), C (interrogation), D (quotation), E (asymmetrical closure), G (withholding, witness relation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-as-01 assailant composite

```text
handle:           Assailant
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       the decorum of a lovers' quarrel laid over an assault; a stranger known only by a description
```

**Position.** The speaker addresses the man who assaulted her. She cannot know him, and the poem will not speak of the assault in the register of fear. It speaks in the register of a failed relationship, which claims an intimacy the facts deny.

**Surface.** A letter in the second person, first person singular throughout, in the idiom of a couple's quarrel and relationship advice. An opening claim of complete, instantaneous knowledge of the man's life and family. The attack reported in the vocabulary of a romantic misunderstanding. Proposals of mutual adjustment. A close in which her actual knowledge turns out to be a height and an item of clothing, given to the police, and her fear turns out to be that she will forget him among other men. Line breaks not read; see Provenance.

**Fold.** The courtship register lets the speaker say the unsayable by misfiling it, and the misfiling is the accusation: the idiom of relationship fits the assault well enough to indict the idiom. The close retracts the claimed knowledge, so the reader who accepted her omniscience learns she has only a police description, and the fear kept out of the register arrives at last as the fear of losing even that.

**Active site.** The close, where claimed knowledge shrinks to a description and the fear of forgetting surfaces.

**Forbidden.** Fear stated in its own register. Rage. The assault narrated as assault. Any real knowledge of the man. A reply.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the man; she has a description and a few seconds. He will not read the letter. The register cannot say what she felt, and she does not leave it.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: the assault enters speech only disguised as intimacy, and the disguise is glib by her own admission. Whether the poem reports an event in the poet's life is unshown (P4); Register Is Not Referent keeps the real and invented readings both live, and the fold needs neither. Transferred to a draft where the violence is not real on the page, the courtship register becomes a joke about dating. Transferred to one where the violence is real and the register is not sustained, it becomes bravado.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-as-02 through cf-as-04. The specimen also runs a variant of cf-et-02, retracted access: the speaker narrates the man's history and family with full confidence and then discloses that her knowledge is a police description. See the recorded recurrences in Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default, where the glib register would launder the assault if the close did not retract it. Body-cost rule, since the attack is carried in the speaker's breath and throat.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, first/second-person management), C (interrogation), D (irony), E (closure), G (credibility management, tonal laundering).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-wh-01 white lady composite

```text
handle:           White Lady
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             upward
imposed by:       a power that holds the community's children and speaks only to them
```

**Position.** A community of parents addresses the power that has taken its children. The power speaks only to the children, in seduction, and the parents can only ask what it would cost to get them back, a question the power does not answer.

**Surface.** A subtitle glossing the title as street slang for a drug. Lowercase throughout and almost unpunctuated. Three movements: the power's appetite for named children, set beside slavery; the power's own seduction, quoted in its voice as it speaks to the young; the community's address to the power, a litany of charges with the title phrase returning at line ends, closing on questions of price. Line breaks not read; this corpus joins the first movement. See Provenance.

**Fold.** The double name makes a drug and a plantation mistress one figure, so the present harm arrives carrying the logic of the old one: the children are held as property, and the only speech left to the community is to ask the price, which is the language of sale. The question meant to rescue them repeats the terms of their capture, and the poem ends on it, unanswered, with ownership as its final term.

**Active site.** The closing questions, where return is asked for in the vocabulary of purchase.

**Forbidden.** An answer from the power. The children's voices. Blame of the children. The drug named clinically outside the subtitle. Hope.

**Knowledge test.** The community cannot know the price, because the power sets none and answers only the children. It cannot reach its children except through the power that holds them.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the community's: named children lost, and a history that makes the loss legible as a second enslavement. The subtitle's gloss is on the page (P0). First publication was not checked this pass. Transferred to material with no history behind the power, the double name loses its second term and the price question becomes a plea. A trial by a writer outside the community whose children are named should run the kernel's Appropriation Risk directive before it starts.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-wh-02 through cf-wh-04.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default, since a reading that keeps the drug and drops the slavery has laundered the poem. Appropriation Risk, before any trial.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, apostrophe), B (refrain, epistrophe), C (interrogation), D (personification), G (appropriation risk).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-no-01 norway composite

```text
handle:           Norway
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       a place of origin none of them has seen, reachable only by leaving
```

**Position.** A son urges his aging parents toward the country their family came from. None of them has seen it. The parents never board and gradually forget what the place was, and the son can learn it only by going himself, which parts him from them.

**Surface.** First person past. Reported dialogue in which the family name and the place-name are repeated as reasons. One scene at a dock, ships leaving in one direction. The parents' decline told through their stillness and their forgetting. A reversal in which the son announces his own departure in the words his parents had used. A close across widening water. The place-name is never glossed. Line breaks not read; see Provenance.

**Fold.** Because the destination is never glossed and the story never leaves the literal, the place-name gathers every meaning the reader brings to a departure no one returns from, origin, adulthood, death, and the poem asserts none of them. The reversal at the end puts the reader's meaning under pressure: whatever the place is, the child reaches it first, and the parents, who talked of it all their lives, are left waving.

**Active site.** The son's announcement, in his parents' words, that he has to go now.

**Forbidden.** A gloss. The parents boarding. A report from the place. Tears before the close. Allegorical signals: a capitalized abstraction, a named afterlife.

**Knowledge test.** No one in the poem knows the place. The parents lose even the name of what they wanted. The son can know it only by leaving, and the poem cannot follow him.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is separation: the one who goes cannot tell the ones left behind what he found, and the ones who longed longest never learn. Transferred to a destination the reader decodes at once, the parable becomes an allegory with a key. Transferred to a destination that means nothing to anyone in the poem, the dock scene is whimsy.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-no-02 through cf-no-04.

**Engines admitted.** Load-bearing foreignness, where the family name and the foreign place carry the origin. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (spatial deixis, first/second/third-person management), B (repetition), D (symbol), E (turn, closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ul-01 ultrasound composite

```text
handle:           Ultrasound
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       an image that shows the body and not the person; a womb the father cannot enter
```

**Position.** A father can know his unborn child only through an image that shows every forming part and not the thing he most wants to see. He addresses the child, who cannot hear him, from outside the body that holds it.

**Surface.** Second-person address to the unborn, affectionate and joking, with pet names. Advice deferred. An inventory of body parts in formation, anatomical and exact. A question about where the soul is and when it arrives. A close in which the father places himself outside, the mother inside, and predicts the child will know him by a feature they share. Short lines that appear intact in this file; nothing here turns on them.

**Fold.** The image's completeness locates its lack. The inventory is exhaustive, so the absence of the one thing it cannot show is exact, and the father's question about the soul lands inside a list that has accounted for everything else. His exclusion then turns to comedy: the mother already knows the child without seeing it, and he will be recognized only by inheritance, which moves the recognition he wants from the soul to a family feature.

**Active site.** The question about the soul, set inside the inventory.

**Forbidden.** A soul found. Sentiment without the joke. The mother's voice. The child answering.

**Knowledge test.** The father cannot see the soul or know when it comes, and the child cannot see or hear him. The image shows him everything except what he is looking for.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the father's exclusion: he can reach his child only through an image and an address that cannot arrive. Transferred to a speaker with no stake in the child, the inventory is a biology lesson. Transferred without the comedy, the soul question becomes piety.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ul-02 and cf-ul-03. The specimen also runs two earlier primitives in variant: cf-he-02, address to the unborn, made tender rather than prophetic, and cf-mt-03, instrument limit, where an imaging device shows the body at exactly the point where it cannot show the person. See the recorded recurrences in Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Defamiliarization engine, where the anatomical names make the child strange enough to be seen. Body-cost rule.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, apostrophe, zoom by noun specificity), C (list syntax, interrogation), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-wi-01 widow composite

```text
handle:           Widow
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       an absence the speaker cannot see into; an hour's lateness
```

**Position.** A wife waits for a husband who is an hour late. She cannot know where he is or whether he is alive, and she fills the interval with his deaths, which she then undoes.

**Surface.** Second-person address to the absent husband, present tense. A catalog of imagined accidents, escalating toward the absurd. A plain statement of the actual lateness. A memory of the gift that made the danger possible. Rescues imagined as deliberately as the disasters. A close in which he returns as several of himself. The title names her a widow before anything has happened. Line breaks read from this file only; nothing here turns on them.

**Fold.** Because she cannot know, her imagination runs both ways, killing him and saving him, and the poem treats the two as one labor. The title's premature widowhood is the premise she lives under for the hour, and the surplus husbands at the close are her insurance against the verdict the title has already given.

**Active site.** The close, where he returns multiplied.

**Forbidden.** His arrival confirmed. News of him. Fear stated as fear. Blame.

**Knowledge test.** She cannot know where he is or whether he is alive. She knows only the time.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the waiting itself, spent in imagined bereavement. The comedy works because the fear is real and the lateness trivial. Transferred to a wait with no real risk behind it, it is hysteria played for laughs.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-wi-02 and cf-wi-03.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal, for the dread under the comedy.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position), C (list syntax), E (title as frame, closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-te-01 termination composite

```text
handle:           Termination
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             upward
imposed by:       a management that values speed over the knowledge that keeps workers safe
```

**Position.** A foreman knows the machines through his hands and ears, well enough to foresee their accidents. His knowledge lives in his body and cannot be handed to the managers above him, who want speed and will not wait for it, and it cannot save him from them.

**Surface.** Third person, past tense. One long accumulating sentence for the foreman's listening. A turn to a new manager and an ambitious operator. The pressure registered in the foreman's body as weight, until the body stops. A final sentence praising the men who drove him, in their own idiom. Line breaks not read; see Provenance.

**Fold.** The knowledge that keeps other men's faces intact is the knowledge that makes him slow, so the thing he knows is the thing that kills him. The poem gives no judgment of the managers in its own voice. It closes by praising them in their own terms, and the praise convicts them, because the reader has just watched what their efficiency cost.

**Active site.** The closing sentence of praise.

**Forbidden.** The foreman's speech. Direct condemnation. The managers' point of view rendered with sympathy. Sentiment about the stroke.

**Knowledge test.** The managers cannot know what the foreman knows, since it is in his hands and ears. He cannot transmit it, and it cannot foresee what is happening to him.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the foreman's body: decades of noise in his hands and jaw, then the weight, then the stroke. The poet's own working life is not relied on here. Transferred to work where no body is at risk, the closing irony is office sarcasm.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-te-02 and cf-te-03.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule. Anti-laundering engine default, where a reading that keeps the craft and drops the killing launders the poem.

**Device cluster.** A (third-person management), B (repetition), C (cumulative sentence), D (irony, simile), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-hi-01 history lesson composite

```text
handle:           History Lesson
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a grandmother's silence about the history she arranges; the frame of a photograph
```

**Position.** The speaker reads her childhood through a photograph her grandmother took. The history that explains it is not in the picture. It is in an earlier photograph of the grandmother on the same coast, and in the pose the grandmother, standing behind the camera, tells the child to take.

**Surface.** First person present, describing a photograph: age, place, body, pose, light. The photographer placed outside the frame, directing. Two dates and the interval between them stated flatly. A second photograph described in parallel, the pose repeated in a different garment on a narrower strip of beach, with the segregation marker named once. No commentary. Line breaks not read; see Provenance.

**Fold.** The lesson passes by pose rather than by telling. The grandmother arranges the child's body the way hers was arranged forty years earlier, and the repeated pose carries a history neither photograph can show alone. The child in the picture cannot know what she is repeating, the adult reading it knows only by juxtaposition, and the reader receives the history as a rhyme between two bodies.

**Active site.** The close, where the second photograph repeats the first pose.

**Forbidden.** The grandmother explaining. Commentary on segregation beyond the marker. The child's awareness. Emotion stated.

**Knowledge test.** The child cannot know the history she is posing inside. The photograph cannot show the beach as it was, and the grandmother, who knows, is outside the frame and speaks only instructions.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the grandmother's history, carried in her body and passed to the child as posture. The two dates and the interval are on the page as the poem's claims (P0). Whether the photographs exist is unshown here (P4), and Register Is Not Referent keeps both readings live. Transferred to a draft whose repeated pose carries no history, the rhyme is a family resemblance and nothing more.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-hi-02 and cf-hi-03.

**Engines admitted.** Access-asymmetry, between readers who know the history of segregated beaches and readers who do not. Anti-laundering engine default, where a reading that keeps the beach and drops the segregation launders the poem.
