**Device cluster.** A (speaker position, temporal deixis), C (declarative flatness), E (ekphrastic frame, closure), G (withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ss-01 subtle sight composite

```text
handle:           Subtle Sight
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             inward
imposed by:       a sight that sees through everyone and cannot check itself
```

**Position.** The speaker sees through every surface to the falseness beneath it, others' and his own, and cannot know whether what he sees is the truth about people or a fault in his seeing.

**Surface.** Blank verse in one paragraph, first person, moving between general statement and direct cry. An opening benediction on whoever has this sight, which turns into a curse. A catalog of what the sight strips away: rank, beauty, speech, and at last flesh down to bone. A turn in which the sight falls on the speaker himself. A direct question whether all men are false or the fault lies in the one who sees. A petition to lose the sight, weighing trust that may be betrayed against doubt that may be confirmed, closing on a prayer to see the best in others. Line breaks read from this file only; nothing here turns on them.

**Fold.** A sight that exposes everything has no vantage from which to verify itself, so the catalog of exposures becomes evidence against the seer as much as against the seen. When the speaker turns the sight on himself and finds the same falseness, he can no longer tell whether he is seeing the world or projecting himself onto it. Looking harder cannot settle the question, since looking is the faculty in question, so the poem settles it by asking to be blinded: it prefers a trust that may be betrayed to a doubt that may be true.

**Active site.** The question whether all men are false or the fault lies in the one who sees.

**Forbidden.** A verified instance, someone seen through and proved false. Someone seen through and found good. A cure by argument. Contempt that exempts the speaker.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether his sight is true, because the only instrument for checking it is the sight itself. He knows what he sees and not whether it is there.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: isolation among people he cannot trust, and a self he finds as false as they are. The author is unidentified, and no biographical reading is available or relied on. Transferred to a speaker whose suspicions are cynicism with evidence, the central question is rhetorical and the prayer to be blinded is sentiment.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ss-02 and cf-ss-03. The specimen is the inverse of the catalog's unknowing-register cases: where cf-sc-05 cannot see its own gap, this speaker sees everything, himself included, and cannot verify any of it.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent argument as a phase tension, since the poem debates its own sight in the open.

**Device cluster.** A (first/second/third-person management), C (interrogation, list syntax), D (metaphor), G (self-implication, credibility management).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-pn-01 proud name composite

```text
handle:           Proud Name
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             inward
imposed by:       a pride that forbids confession; a man known only through signs he cannot control
```

**Position.** A woman of old family and no fortune loves a proud man and cannot speak. Her pride forbids her to admit a fault that parts them, his pride answers hers, and afterward everything she learns of him reaches her through glances he cannot control, the village's rumor, one letter, and the reports of others, until his death.

**Surface.** A sequence of lyrics in the woman's first person, in varied stanzas with refrains, following one story in order: a first meeting; doubt of her own worth; avowal and betrothal; a quarrel over a fault she will not own; his letter announcing a marriage made in pique; a year of public pretense; the wife's flight and his disgrace; a farewell; a fatal fall from his horse, reported to her by others; and her refusal to wish him back. Parenthetical asides carry what she will not say aloud. His eyes are twice said to betray his will. The file carries no titles or boundaries for the sequence's lyrics, and several stanzas appear to lack a line. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** Because neither will speak, the sequence moves its knowledge into involuntary channels: eyes that betray a will, asides that betray the speaker, rumor that betrays a house. The reader learns the story the way she does, through leaks, and the one direct utterance between the lovers, his letter, arrives only after speech can change nothing. The proud silence that caused the loss becomes the form in which the loss is told.

**Active site.** The lyric in which she knows the fault is hers, will not say so, and cannot name what stops her.

**Forbidden.** A spoken confession between them before the letter. Her direct account of his feelings, rather than her reading of his eyes. Reconciliation. Blame settled.

**Knowledge test.** She cannot know his heart except through signs he is trying to suppress, and she cannot name what forbids her own confession. After the quarrel she cannot know his life except by rumor and report.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is two lives by the speaker's own count, and the silence is hers by her own account. The author is unidentified; see Provenance for the internal leads, which the entry does not rest on. Transferred to a quarrel with nothing at stake, the proud silence is sulking.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-pn-02 through cf-pn-04. The sequence also runs two earlier primitives in variant: cf-wa-03, split display, in her year of public laughter over private despair, and cf-me-04, borrowed testimony, since his death reaches her only as others report it. It closes on a borrowed royal lament, a wish to have died in the other's place, the same wish that closes Yonder Hill. See the recorded recurrences in Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface, where the asides carry what the lyric surface will not. Compartmentalization, where the public display walls off the grief.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position), B (refrain), C (parenthesis, interrogation), E (serial sequence), G (withholding, credibility management).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-kh-01 known heaven composite

```text
handle:           Known Heaven
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             upward
imposed by:       a God who alone judges between two women; a wife who has cast the speaker out
```

**Position.** A woman cast into the desert with her child addresses God. She has lost the man she loved to his wife, she admits her own sin, and she cannot know how God judges between her and the wife who drove them out, or whether he will answer at all.

**Surface.** Sixteen lines in four quatrains, envelope-rhymed and then alternating; check it. An exclamation of fall from heaven into hell. A question whether exile is not hell. A prayer for mercy figured as a descending bird. A petition to be struck dead with her child rather than watch the child starve. A confession, a refusal to curse the wife, and a closing question to God: which of the two is guiltier. The biblical figure is never named; the speaker is identifiable by allusion (P4). Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The confession and the refusal to curse strip the speaker of every claim but the question. Having admitted her guilt and withheld her curse, she has nothing left to plead with, so the closing question is put without advocacy, and it becomes a charge laid before the only judge, with the answer left to him. The poem ends on the verdict it cannot know and has made impossible to evade.

**Active site.** The closing question.

**Forbidden.** God's answer. A curse. Self-justification. The child's voice.

**Knowledge test.** She cannot know God's judgment between them, or whether he will answer. She knows her own sin and the wife's act.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's and her child's: exile, hunger, and a death she asks for rather than watch the child suffer. The identification with the woman sent into the desert with her son in Genesis rests on allusion (P4). Transferred to a speaker with no real judge above her, the closing question is an accusation dressed as humility.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-kh-02 and cf-kh-03. The specimen also runs a variant of cf-cl-04, self-convicting reason: the admission against herself is given flat before the question about the verdict.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule, since the child's hunger is where the cost lands. Transparent argument as a phase tension.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, apostrophe), C (interrogation, imperative), E (closure), G (self-implication).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-pd-01 path of dreams composite

```text
handle:           Path of Dreams
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             outward
imposed by:       the far ends of three paths, the last of which no one returns from
```

**Position.** The poem follows three paths in turn, the path of poetry, the path of sleep, and the path of death, and knows each only up to the point where its travelers cannot report back. Of the third it knows only that no one returns.

**Surface.** Blank verse in three parallel paragraphs. Each opens beside a stream running toward a sea, and the stream darkens from one paragraph to the next, from singing to silent to the river of forgetting. Each describes who walks the path, what they meet, and what they know. The first ends in confidence, the second in certain waking, the third in certain non-return, with the destination left undecided between paradise and underworld. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The parallel frame makes each path a stage in one argument about knowledge: the poet's path is known to lead upward, the sleeper's to lead back, and the dead man's only not to lead back. As the paths darken, the one certainty narrows from destination to return to non-return, so the poem arrives at death holding only the knowledge that it cannot follow. The repeated frame is the argument, and the variation at each close is the finding.

**Active site.** The close of the third movement, where the only certainty is that no one comes back.

**Forbidden.** A report from the far shore. A choice between the two destinations. Comfort beyond the stated certainty. A break in the parallel frame.

**Knowledge test.** The poem cannot know where the third path ends, only that it has no return. It knows the first two only through the travelers who come back.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is knowledge running out exactly where it matters: the dead are met in dreams and lost at waking, and beyond that the poem has nothing. Transferred without the two earlier paths, the closing certainty is a commonplace; the structure is what gives it weight.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-pd-02 and cf-pd-03. The third movement runs a variant of cf-ap-04, unclassified state, holding the destination level between two kinds, and the second and third together repeat Apple-Picking's pairing of sleep and death.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal. Load-bearing foreignness, where the classical underworld names carry the far shore.

**Device cluster.** B (repetition), C (parallelism), D (allegory), E (serial sequence, repetition with variation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-yh-01 yonder hill composite

```text
handle:           Yonder Hill
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a dead rival who cannot be answered; a husband's heart the wife can read only through her fear
```

**Position.** A second wife cannot know whether her husband still loves the first, who is dead and buried nearby. The rival cannot be met, changed, or outlived, and the husband's love can be read only through the wife's fear.

**Surface.** Five quatrains in pentameter, envelope-rhymed; check it. An opening question that dismisses the dead woman's silence, answered by a confession of fear. A general maxim about first love. The dead woman felt as an interference in the couple's embraces. The dead woman found in the flowers and seasons. A close in which the wife names her own status, restates the fear, and wishes she were the dead woman. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** A living rival can be answered and a dead one cannot, so the wife's contest has no terms: the dead woman is fixed at the moment she was loved, and every present intimacy is measured against a memory the wife cannot see into. Unable to know her husband's heart, she reads it through her fear, and the fear gives the rival a presence in everything. The closing wish completes the logic: the only way to win is to lose everything the living wife has.

**Active site.** The closing wish.

**Forbidden.** The husband's voice. Evidence of his feeling beyond her fear. The dead woman's voice. Resolution.

**Knowledge test.** She cannot know whether he still loves the first wife. She knows only her fear and the signs it reads into the world, and the dead woman cannot be known or answered.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the wife's: a marriage she cannot believe in, against a rival death has made perfect. A possible biographical referent exists if the author is who the internal evidence suggests (P4; see Provenance), and the entry does not rest on it. Transferred to a rival who could be met, the fear is jealousy with remedies.

### cf-nh-01 not hear composite

```text
handle:           Not Hear
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             outward
imposed by:       death; an attention the dead now give elsewhere
```

**Position.** The speaker addresses a beloved in the grave and cannot know whether the dead hear. The poem begins by denying the death and ends by deciding the silence.

**Surface.** Two movements in monorhymed tercets, each closing on a short refrain line. The first refrain asks whether the dead hears. The second repeats the words as an exclamation that the dead does not. The whole is printed in italics. In the first movement the dead is imagined asleep among flowers and birdsong. In the second the speaker imagines every appeal she could make, news of flowers, a song stolen from nature, the old name, and asks whether the dead would stay mute. Check the rhyme scheme; line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The refrain carries the finding: the same words, first as question and then as verdict, and the change of mood is the whole of what the speaker learns. She reaches the verdict not by evidence but by imagining every appeal and its failure. The reason she gives for the silence, that the dead now listen to heavenly music and no longer hold earthly loves dear, consoles and excludes at once: the beloved is well, and the speaker is no longer among the things the beloved cares for.

**Active site.** The second refrain, where the question returns as a statement.

**Forbidden.** An answer from the dead. A ghost. Anger at the silence. Doubt reopened after the verdict.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether the dead hear. The poem decides that they do not, and the decision rests on imagined appeals and an assumed heaven, not on anything the speaker can know.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: the beloved is lost a second time, to a heaven that has changed what the dead care for. The author is unidentified. Transferred to an addressee whose silence has an ordinary explanation, the refrain's turn is a sigh.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-nh-02 and cf-nh-03. The second movement runs a variant of cf-cl-02, exhaustive foreclosure: each appeal the living could make is imagined and closed before the verdict.

**Engines admitted.** Load-bearing foreignness, where an angel of music carries the heaven that excludes her. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, apostrophe), B (end rhyme, refrain), C (interrogation, conditionals), E (refrain shift, closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-pw-01 prisoned within composite

```text
handle:           Prisoned Within
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a body others read as brutish; a world that judges by it
```

**Position.** The speaker holds an inner self no one can see, behind a body the world reads as brutish and low. He cannot show that self to anyone and cannot be loved, and he expects to be seen whole only by God, after death.

**Surface.** Long lines in a loose rhymed scheme. A run of rhetorical questions about what the world can perceive, what he can win, and what he can know of love, each answered by a description of his own body in a vocabulary of contempt, animal and servile. A turn to the soul as a prisoner beating on the body's bars, ending in its release to face God. At least three lines appear lost in this file, by the rhyme scheme; the arc from opening question to close survives. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The speaker describes his body only in the terms of those who reject him, so the poem's claim of an inner self stands against his own words for his outside, and the reader measures how far the world's view has entered him by how fluently he speaks it. Because no living person can see past that body, the only witness the poem can imagine is God after death. The self is known only once the body that hid it is gone.

**Active site.** The first question answered by his own face.

**Forbidden.** A person who sees past the body. Rejection of the world's vocabulary. Hope in this life. Softening the degrading terms, which carry the poem's premise.

**Knowledge test.** No one living can know his inner self, and he cannot know himself except in the terms they use. Only a witness after death will see him.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: exclusion from love, and a self-description dictated by contempt. The poem's premise that a brutish look marks a low person, and its vocabulary of apes and servile birth, draw on physiognomic types of its period that were also used to degrade whole groups. The poem names no group (P0). The entry keeps that premise visible under the kernel's anti-laundering default rather than reading the poem as a generic lyric of the hidden self. Transferred to a speaker whose appearance costs nothing socially, the lament is vanity. Transferred with the degrading vocabulary aimed at a real group, it reproduces the contempt it describes.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-pw-02 and cf-pw-03. The close runs a variant of cf-mc-03, posthumous verdict: the one full witness arrives after the body is gone.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default. Body-cost rule.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position), C (interrogation, apposition), D (metaphor), G (self-implication, appropriation risk).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-og-01 outer gate composite

```text

rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             upward

```

**Position.** The speaker knows love only from outside, as a starving man outside a palace knows a banquet: by what escapes the walls. He may not enter, and the power that keeps him out is named only as Fate.

**Surface.** A sonnet. The octave is an extended simile: a starving man at a palace gate sees light through curtains, hears song, smells the feast, and waits in the cold, grateful for any scrap. **Fold.** The simile makes the exclusion bodily, a hunger, and gives the speaker's knowledge an exact shape: everything he knows of love leaks through walls as light, sound, and scent, which gives him the feast's outline and denies him its substance. The octave's gratitude for scraps measures how little the lover will get, and the sestet does not even give him the scrap.

**Active site.** The sestet's statement that he may not enter.

**Forbidden.** Entry. A named excluder beyond Fate. A scrap received. Resentment of those within.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know love from inside. He knows only what leaks out of it.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is hunger made into figure: an exclusion with no remedy. Transferred to a speaker whose exclusion is temporary or chosen, the simile is self-pity.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-og-02 and cf-og-03.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule.

**Device cluster.** A (spatial deixis, speaker position), D (simile, conceit), E (sonnet architecture, turn).

### cf-lp-01 love passed by composite

```text
handle:           Love Passed By
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             inward
imposed by:       a love specified in advance from songs and stories; a heart trusted to announce it
```

**Position.** The speaker has specified the beloved in advance, from the love-lore of song and story, and trusts the heart to announce love when it comes. Love comes in another shape, the speaker does not recognize it, and learns what passed only after it is gone.

**Surface.** Eight quatrains in four-stress lines with feminine rhymes; check it. A youth's dream of love. The prescription, spoken as a vow in quotation marks: the beloved's hands, eyes, hair, lips, and face, each fixed in advance, with a parenthesis promising that the heart will announce her. A long wait into dark. A woman passes who matches none of it, and drops a flower at the speaker's feet. A second parenthesis accuses the heart. A close in which the speaker realizes, after she has gone, what passed. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The prescription is so detailed that the reader holds it like a checklist and sees her fail every item, and sees the dropped flower, before the speaker does. The poem's knowledge reaches the speaker last, in the final line, where the gap between reader and speaker closes only when closing it is useless. The heart the speaker trusted to know turns out to have been trained by the same songs that wrote the prescription.

**Active site.** The dropped flower, and the final line's recognition.

**Forbidden.** Recognition in time. Her return. Her voice. A second chance.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot recognize a love that does not match the prescription, and the faculty trusted to know it fails. The speaker learns only after she is gone.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the love missed, and the speaker's own verdict closes the poem. Transferred to a speaker who prescribed nothing, the miss is bad luck. The prescription is what makes it a confinement.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-lp-02 and cf-lp-03. The specimen runs a variant of cf-jc-03, unread evidence: the dropped flower sits on the page unread until too late. Its two parentheses repeat the book's device from Proud Name.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position), B (end rhyme), C (parenthesis, parallelism), D (allusion), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ws-01 winding sheet composite

```text
handle:           Winding Sheet
rights ceiling:   Level 4 available, held at Level 0
axis:             outward
imposed by:       a death from inside which the speaker cannot speak; a love the mourners do not know
```

**Position.** The speaker narrates her own laying-out from inside death. The mourners believe her gone and cannot know she is aware. She cannot tell them, and cannot tell them about the man she thinks of, whose roses are laid on her heart by someone who does not know what they mean.

**Surface.** Short stanzas in pentameter, with a shortened line at each turn. One stanza has a line fewer than the others in this file. The laying-out and the mourners' whispered verdict, reported from inside. Her awareness through the night, fixed on one man, who is marked only by an italicized pronoun. In the morning, someone who loved her lays on her heart the roses that man sent. A final line of gladness at death, unexplained. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** Speaking from inside death makes the reader the only one who hears her, so the reader alone knows two things the mourners do not: that she is aware, and whom the roses are from. The final gladness is left unexplained, and the reader has to supply its reason from what only the reader knows, either the love confirmed at last or the relief of being beyond it. The poem holds both.

**Active site.** The final line.

**Forbidden.** The mourners learning. The man appearing. An explanation of the gladness. A return to life.

**Knowledge test.** The mourners cannot know she is aware or what the roses mean. The reader cannot know why she is glad.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is a love that could be acknowledged only at death and is kept secret through it. Transferred to a speaker with no secret, the post-mortem frame is a gothic device.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ws-02 and cf-ws-03. The specimen runs a variant of cf-mc-03, posthumous verdict: the token of the man's love reaches her only in death.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface, where an italicized pronoun carries the secret the mourners cannot read. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position), D (quotation), E (closure), F (typographic emphasis), G (withholding).

### cf-pu-01 punctum composite

```text
handle:           Punctum
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             upward
imposed by:       a custodial voice that has retouched the image; a victim removed from his own photograph
```

**Position.** The viewer looks at a photograph from which the victim of a lynching has been removed. The image is presented by a plural voice that steers the viewer toward the landscape and away from what is missing, and the viewer can know the dead man only by the shadow he left.

**Surface.** A prose block after an epigraph giving a museum's count of poorly documented lynchings in one state across a century. Second-person address to the viewer, interleaved with a plural voice that tells the viewer where to look, always at the pleasant parts of the scene. The voice insists nothing is hidden, explains the shadow away as a bird or a flaw in the camera, admits to retouching, and invites the viewer to move on. The viewer's attention returns each time to a shadow on the ground attached to no one. A turn in which the viewer touches their own throat. Prose; no line breaks to read.

**Fold.** The guiding voice does the erasure's work in speech. Every direction it gives points toward what remains, so the viewer's repeated return to the shadow is disobedience, and the reader learns the victim exists only by resisting the voice that presents the picture. The shadow is the one piece of evidence the retouching could not remove, and the viewer's hand at the throat puts the viewer where the missing body was. The poem cannot restore the man. It can only make looking at his absence an act against the custodians.

**Active site.** The viewer's hand moving to the throat.

**Forbidden.** The victim restored or named. The custodians answered directly. Description of the violence. A verdict on the custodians in the poem's own voice.

**Knowledge test.** The viewer cannot know the victim: he has been removed, and the voice that controls the image denies there is anything to know. The only evidence is a shadow the voice explains away.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the dead man's second erasure, and the viewer's complicity in looking where the voice points. The book's notes state that the poem was inspired by Ken Gonzales-Day's Erased Lynchings series (2006), held by the Smithsonian American Art Museum (P1: the notes name the work and its accession). Transferred to an image whose absence harms no one, the guiding voice is a museum tour and the defiance has no object.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-pu-02 and cf-pu-03. The specimen inverts cf-hi-02, photographer outside the frame: here the voice outside the frame curates the history out of the picture rather than posing it in. The shadow runs a variant of cf-mt-02, obscured dispatch.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default, since a reading that keeps the sunlit landscape and drops the lynching repeats what the custodial voice does. Appropriation Risk, before any trial.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, pronoun shift), C (imperative, reported speech), E (ekphrastic frame), G (withholding, appropriation risk).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ah-01 amazon history composite

```text
handle:           Amazon History
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       a death; a record that can hold only purchases
```

**Position.** A woman's last years can be known only through a retailer's record of what she bought. The record has no voice, no feeling, and no commentary, and it goes on after she dies.

**Surface.** A list organized by month, each month followed by product entries in the record's own format: brand names, quantities, sizes. No speaker, no sentence, no commentary; the title is the only frame. Some months are empty. The items move from work supplies and small comforts to pain relief, then to medical aids, then to memorial goods, and a few entries continue after the memorial purchases. One entry per line, in the record's layout.

**Fold.** Because the poem can say only what a purchase record says, every feeling has to be inferred from quantities, repetitions, and gaps, and the reader does the grieving the record cannot. The recurring painkiller entry works as a refrain, the empty months are the silences, and the entries that continue after the memorial goods turn the record from her life into someone else's grief. The document's indifference is the form of the loss.

**Active site.** The month in which the entries turn from comforts to medical aids.

**Forbidden.** Commentary. A speaker. Any entry that is not a purchase. A caption for the empty months.

**Knowledge test.** The reader cannot know what she felt, what the empty months held, or who made the last purchases. The record knows only what was bought.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is carried entirely by inference: a working life, an illness, a death, and a survivor, readable only in a store's ledger. The book is dedicated to the poet's mother by name, and a mother is named elsewhere in the book (P0). Whether this record is hers, or a real record at all, is unshown (P4), and Register Is Not Referent keeps both readings live. Transferred to the record of a life that did not end or cost anyone, the list is a shopping list.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ah-02 through cf-ah-04. The specimen runs a variant of cf-jc-02, remains as record, and it is the catalog's first poem made entirely of found document, the operation cf-wl-02 performs for a single line. Its title-as-only-frame recurs in Old Glory, in the same book.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule, since the body's decline is legible in the purchases. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** C (list syntax), E (serial sequence), F (document format), G (withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ol-01 old glory composite

```text
handle:           Old Glory
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a national idiom in which praise, sex, and laughter are spoken as violence
```

**Position.** The poem can speak only in the idioms a culture uses for success, sex, and humor, and nearly every one of them is an idiom of killing, bombing, or assault. No speaker steps outside the idiom to say so.

**Surface.** Fourteen short lines of stacked idioms and catchphrases in several unnamed voices: praise, boasts, come-ons, laughter, each built on a violent metaphor. Enjambment splits some idioms across the line. A slur and violent sexual idioms appear in the voices. No commentary. The title names the national flag. The lines appear intact in this file; check the enjambments against the book.

**Fold.** Stacking the idioms without comment lets their literal violence surface by accumulation, so the reader hears what the speakers do not: the language of approval is the language of war and assault. The title supplies the only frame and turns the collage into a portrait of a nation by its figures of speech. The last idiom makes death the figure for delight, and the poem stops on it.

**Active site.** The closing idiom, death as the word for joy.

**Forbidden.** Commentary. A speaker who hears the violence. An idiom without a violent root. A gloss on the title.

**Knowledge test.** The voices cannot hear the violence in what they say, since the idioms are dead metaphors to their users. The poem cannot step outside the idiom to point it out.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is borne by the people the idioms target, some named by slur, and the voices bear none of it. The slur and the violent sexual idioms must stay at Level 0 in any use; a trial that reproduces them reproduces the harm the poem exhibits. Transferred to a field of idioms with no history of real violence behind it, the collage is wordplay.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ol-02 and cf-ol-03. The specimen runs cf-sc-05, unknowing register, at the scale of a language: speakers who cannot hear what their register carries.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default. Transparent ugliness, where the ugliness is displayed and not excused.

**Device cluster.** B (repetition, catalog rhythm), C (parataxis, list syntax), D (dead metaphor), G (tonal laundering, appropriation risk).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sh-01 shaking hands composite

```text

rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only

```

**Position.** A stranger notices the speaker's hands are shaking and asks what happened to him. He did not know he was shaking, cannot find what happened, and spends the rest of the poem searching his life for a cause his body seems to know and he does not.

**Surface.** A short dialogue of flat questions and answers with a newspaper vendor, set as separate lines, then a prose paragraph of aftermath. An interruption by a song from an open window, remembered as pleasure and lost as a title. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Forbidden.** The cause found. A diagnosis. Self-pity. Further involvement from the vendor.

The book elsewhere places its speaker in a hospital ward and on medication (P0 as the book's claims), which gives the shaking a context this poem withholds. ### cf-ps-01 passed a soul composite

```text
handle:           Passed a Soul
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             inward

```

**Position.** During the hour of his mother's funeral the speaker is elsewhere, clearing her overgrown flowerbeds. He uses the work to keep from feeling, asking, or wondering, and the poem keeps his grief as far from its sentences as he keeps it from his mind.

**Surface.** A prose block in the past tense. The labor rendered in close detail. One sentence stating the work's purpose. A stray animal watching. The funeral happens offstage, and the unanswered telephone is its only intrusion. The reader has to supply the grief from the thoroughness of the tidying, which is the only form of mourning the speaker allows himself.

**Forbidden.** The funeral described. Tears. An explanation of his absence. Answering the phone. A memory of the mother.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker will not let himself know his grief, and the poem keeps it out of every sentence but one. The reader cannot know why he stayed away.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's absence from his mother's funeral and the grief he forbids himself. The book returns to the mother's death in two other poems (P0), which the entry does not need. Transferred to a speaker with no reason to stay away, the tidying is avoidance without cost.

The specimen also inverts cf-sm-03, silent accompaniment: absent from the rite with the body busy, rather than present with the voice withheld.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule. Compartmentalization, where the work walls off the grief.

### cf-ea-01 enueg i composite

```text

rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only

**Position.** The speaker has left the bedside of a woman who is dying and walks across a city and out along a canal. He cannot bear what he has left, and the poem's attention goes to everything on the walk except the room it came from, which appears only in its first lines.

**Surface.** Long and short free-verse lines tracing one walk past named places. The child who will not enter the field because he was put out once before is the speaker's position in small, a knowledge learned by exclusion. The last word goes to a quoted vision rather than to the room.

**Forbidden.** A return to the sickroom. The woman's name or face. Grief stated. Pity for the speaker.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot face the dying he left, so the poem can know it only as displacement. What he passes is seen; what he fled is not looked at.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's flight, and the poem's refusal to look back is the flight continued as form. Biographical identifications of the dying woman circulate and are not relied on (P4). Transferred to a walk away from nothing, the catalog is ill temper.

### cf-eb-01 enueg ii composite

rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only

```

**Position.** Lying on a bridge at evening, the speaker faces a face that is crumbling into the sky, and cannot speak of the dying. The proverb that permits only good to be said of the dead is shifted to the dying and breaks off before its permission.

**Surface.** Short unpunctuated lines. One word repeated four times to open. The altered proverb, truncated. The face dissolving into the evening by degrees. The plea asks for the one legendary image of a face that survives its bearer, and the poem has none: the face it has is already going. The foreign assurance at the close insists on something the poem never says.

**Active site.** The truncated proverb.

**Forbidden.** Completing the proverb. Naming the dying. A face that holds. Translating the assurance.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot say what may be said of the dying, and cannot keep the face he sees. He knows only that it is going.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is a grief that decorum and exhaustion together silence. Transferred with no dying person behind it, the truncation is a Latin joke.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-eb-02 and cf-eb-03. The truncated formula recurs in the same author's Sanies II, which closes on a liturgical line missing its last word.

### cf-al-01 alba composite

```text
handle:           Alba
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       a beloved whose beauty discloses nothing beyond itself
```

**Position.** The speaker awaits a beloved who will come before morning and bring all the meaning a love poem expects. She comes, and her beauty stands before him as a covering that states only itself, so nothing is revealed and he is left with himself and a dead weight.

**Surface.** A dawn song in free verse, future tense turning to present. A catalog of what she will bring: a poet, a theology, strata and mysteries, the moon, a music. A run of eastern images. A concession that her compassion cannot add to her bounty. A turn in which her beauty becomes an opaque surface, followed by three negations that deny in turn illumination, disclosure, and sacrament. A three-part close. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The dawn song's promise is inverted by its own logic: the more fully her beauty arrives, the less it points past itself, so the revelation the genre expects at dawn becomes a covering. The three negations strip the poem of its light, its disclosure, and its sacrament in one line, and what remains is a speaker alone before a surface. The beloved's presence is the limit.

**Active site.** The three negations.

**Forbidden.** A disclosure. Her speech. Daylight. Consummation.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know anything through her beyond her beauty, which states only itself. The revelation he was promised is unavailable in her presence.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: the loved presence becomes the barrier to what it promised. Transferred to a beloved who could simply be known, the covering is sulking.

### cf-ma-01 malacoda composite

```text
handle:           Malacoda
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       the professional handling of a father's body; a mother whose seeing the son must ration
```

**Position.** At his father's funeral the son can neither stop the undertaker's men nor spare his mother. He can only manage what she hears and sees as the body is measured, coffined, and covered, and the rations shrink until she must see.

**Surface.** Short unpunctuated lines. The undertaker's man on three visits, named for a devil of Dante's hell, his paid composure noted. A question about necessity and its repeated answer. A refrain of permissions for the mother, each return tightening one term until both hearing and seeing are compelled. Instructions to distract her with the garden. A still-life laid on the coffin, whose painted insect the son identifies with his father. A crew's shout of assent and a closing one-word refusal. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The refrain of permissions is a countdown: each variation takes away one protection, so the reader watches the son's power over his mother's knowledge shrink stage by stage while the professionals proceed. The father appears only as a painted insect on the lid, the one figure of transformation in a poem of procedure. The final refusal answers the crew's cheerful assent and can change nothing, which is why it is the last word.

**Active site.** The last variation of the refrain, where she must see.

**Forbidden.** The father's body described. The mother's voice. Grief stated. The undertakers condemned in the poem's own voice.

**Knowledge test.** The son cannot keep his mother from knowing and cannot stop what must happen. He controls only the order in which she learns it, and that control runs out.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the son's helplessness before a procedure, and his mother's exposure to it. The biographical reading, the poet's father's funeral, is widely held and not relied on (P4). Transferred to a death handled with no one to protect, the refrain loses its countdown.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ma-02 through cf-ma-04.

**Engines admitted.** Load-bearing foreignness, where the Dantean names carry the family's view of the job. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** B (refrain with variation), C (imperative), D (allusion), E (closure), G (withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-dt-01 da tagte es composite

rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward

**Position.** The speaker addresses someone who has died and cannot say goodbye to him: the goodbyes went to substitutes. The only certainty of the death is an instrument's null result.

**Surface.** Four lines, envelope-rhymed; check it. An imperative asking the dead to make good the substitute farewells. A departure figured as a sea leave-taking by someone done with land. **Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot say goodbye to the person, only to substitutes, and knows the death only by the absence of breath on glass.

### cf-ec-01 echo's bones composite

```text
handle:           Echo's Bones
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       the grave; a judgment only the consumers of the dead can make
```

**Position.** The speaker walks over the dead all day. Having run the gauntlet of reason and unreason, they are finally taken for what they are, and the only ones who take them so are the creatures that eat them.

**Surface.** Five lines, unpunctuated. The ground as a refuge under the speaker's step. The dead heard at a muffled festivity as the flesh goes. A breaking of wind without favor. The gauntlet run. A last line assigning the true judgment of the dead to the creatures that consume them. The title names the myth of the nymph whose body turned to stone and left only her voice. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** Assigning the one accurate judgment to the consumers of the dead turns all living judgment into the gauntlet the dead have escaped. The speaker, walking above, is still in it: he can hear the dead only as muffled festivity, and he cannot take them for what they are. The title's myth adds that what survives is an echo, and the poem is one.

**Active site.** The last line.

**Forbidden.** A living judge who sees the dead truly. Consolation. The speaker's grief.

**Knowledge test.** The living cannot know the dead for what they are. Only the creatures that consume them do.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the living's permanent misjudgment of the dead, the speaker's included. Transferred to a poem with nothing at stake in judging the dead, it is graveyard humor.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ec-02 and cf-ec-03. The specimen runs a variant of cf-mt-04, sole custodian: the only party with true knowledge of the dead is the one consuming them.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

### cf-ca-01 cascando composite

```text
handle:           Cascando
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             inward
imposed by:       a language of love worn out by use; a lover who cannot tell feeling from pretense
```

**Position.** The speaker wants to speak love and has only words worn past meaning. He cannot tell whether he loves, knows, or pretends, and his being loved depends on another person in a way he can state only as a condition.

**Surface.** Three numbered parts, the third a single line. Short unpunctuated lines, questions without question marks. Part one weighs whether a poor occasion for words is better than none, the heavy hours after the beloved leaves, the dredging up of old loves, and proverbial spans of time that never raised the loved. Part two is a litany of saying again: a series of last times without end, paired conditionals of being loved and loving, a judgment that the words are worn out, then fears in widening order, of not loving, of loving someone else, of being loved by someone else, and of being unable to tell knowledge from ignorance from pretense. Part three is one conditional clause with no main clause. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The poem admits its words are worn out and goes on using them, so the reader hears love spoken in the only language left for it, a language the speaker distrusts as he speaks it. The fears widen until the last is the fear of pretense, which the poem cannot rule out in itself. The final part isolates a condition: the sentence cannot close, because its main clause would be a certainty about being loved that no one in the poem has.

**Active site.** The repeated fear that he cannot tell knowledge from ignorance from pretense.

**Forbidden.** A fresh word for love. A declaration without condition. The beloved's reply. Certainty about his own feeling.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether he loves or pretends, or whether he will be loved. He can state both only as conditions.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: a love he cannot verify in himself, in words he cannot trust. Biographical readings are not relied on. Transferred to a speaker confident of his feeling, the doubt is a pose.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ca-02 through cf-ca-04. The specimen stands to Subtle Sight as feeling to sight: a faculty that cannot verify itself from inside.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent argument as a phase tension. Complaint loop, in the litany of saying again.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position), B (repetition, anaphora), C (conditionals), E (serial sequence, open ending).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-oo-01 ooftish composite

```text
handle:           Ooftish
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             upward
imposed by:       an institution that collects suffering and returns only a formula
```

**Position.** A collector addresses the sufferer, demands every affliction, pools them all, and promises to make sense of them, and the sense returned is one worn formula. The poem gives the sufferer no other account of his suffering.

**Surface.** One stanza in the collector's voice, imperative throughout, brisk and colloquial. Diseases and griefs solicited by name, sentiment dismissed, everything assured a place in one common store to be put to use. A turn that states the sufferer's condition as identity: it is he, and he can neither cure nor bear it. A last line reducing everything to the formula of sacrificial blood. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The collector's generosity is total and indiscriminate: nothing is too small, everything is welcome, all of it goes in together, and that indifference is what makes the offer monstrous. Stating the sufferer's condition as identity shuts every exit but the common store, so the only sense available is the collector's, and the last line shows what that sense is. The poem lets the institution speak until its own final phrase convicts it.

**Active site.** The closing formula.

**Forbidden.** The sufferer's voice. A dissenting voice. A suffering treated as particular. Satire outside the collector's own words.

**Knowledge test.** The sufferer cannot make sense of his suffering, and the only account on offer reduces all suffering to one phrase. The collector does not hear what he says.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the sufferer's, whose afflictions are taken and returned as doctrine. Transferred to an institution with no claim on anyone's suffering, the collector is a joke. Transferred in a living faith's language to its believers, the satire needs the anti-laundering default to keep its target visible.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-oo-02 and cf-oo-03. The specimen runs a variant of cf-pu-02, custodial voice: an institutional voice whose directions trace what they hide.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default. Transparent ugliness. Body-cost rule.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position), B (catalog rhythm), C (imperative, list syntax), D (irony), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-vu-01 vulture composite

```text
handle:           Vulture
rights ceiling:   in copyright, Level 0, structure only
axis:             inward
imposed by:       a hunger that can feed only on the dead, inside the mind that houses it
```

**Position.** The speaker's mind is a scavenger circling inside his own head, stooping to figures that look dead and will rise again. It cannot feed until everything, the hunger included, has become carrion.

**Surface.** Six lines forming one unpunctuated sentence that never completes a main clause. A scavenger bird as the figure of the mind, its sky the inside of the head. A descent toward the prone, who will soon get up and go on living. A mockery by flesh that cannot yet serve. A close deferring the meal until hunger, earth, and sky are all waste. Line breaks read from this file only.

**Fold.** The one sentence never arrives at its main clause, as the hunger never arrives at its meal. The prey keeps rising, so every descent is a mistake, and the scavenger can feed only when nothing, itself included, is left to be hungry. The appetite's satisfaction and its end are the same event, and the poem defers that event past its last word.

**Active site.** The final deferral.

**Forbidden.** A meal. The prey staying dead. A completed main clause. Pity for the prey.

**Knowledge test.** The hunger cannot know which of the prone are dead, since they rise, and it can have its food only when it no longer exists to want it.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: a mind that can take only what has died, in a world that keeps living. Transferred to an appetite that can be met, the scavenger is a mood.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-vu-02 and cf-vu-03.

**Engines admitted.** Defamiliarization engine, where the inside of the head becomes a sky. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** C (syntax without a main clause), D (metaphor), E (open ending).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-fc-01 floating cake composite

```text
handle:           Floating Cake
rights ceiling:   in copyright (translation), Level 0, structure only
axis:             upward
imposed by:       fate and the hands of others that shape a woman's life
```

**Position.** The speaker's shape is decided by others: fate gives her form and fortune, and whatever hands handle her decide how she comes out. She controls nothing of her surface and claims only her center.

**Surface.** Four lines, first person. The speaker's body described as a small food made by hand, its color and form given, its fortunes rising and falling as the food does in water. A concession that others' hands shape her however they will. A closing claim about her center, in terms of color and constancy. The food names the title. The file holds a translation; the original is a quatrain in Tang-regulated form (P4).

**Fold.** Because every surface quality is given to her by fate or by hands, the poem lets them have the surface completely and concedes each point, which makes the final claim unassailable: the center is the one thing the poem never lets the hands reach, and conceding everything else is what proves it. The food's own structure is the argument. What shows is shaped from outside, and what is inside was put there before the shaping began.

**Active site.** The closing claim about the center.

**Forbidden.** Resistance to the shaping. Complaint. A named shaper. The center described beyond its constancy.

**Knowledge test.** Those who shape her know and control only her surface, and cannot reach her center. She cannot control what they make of her.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: a life whose shape others decide, with one claim held back. Most scholars take the poet to have been a concubine, as a published review of this translation reports (P2); the entry rests on the page. Transferred to a speaker whose shape is her own to decide, the claim to a true center is self-regard.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-fc-02 and cf-fc-03. The specimen holds Prisoned Within's position with the valence reversed: an inner self others cannot reach, kept as a possession rather than suffered as a prison.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface, where the food carries the woman. Floor-origin hypothesis, since a street food carries the poem's claim.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position), D (conceit), E (turn, closure), G (credibility management).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ob-01 offering betel composite

handle:           Offering Betel
rights ceiling:   in copyright (translation), Level 0, structure only

**Position.** The speaker offers a man the customary gift of courtship, prepared by her own hand, and cannot know whether their love is fated. The answer will appear only in his mouth, as a color the gift produces or fails to produce.

**Surface.** Four lines. The gift's ingredients named plainly. The speaker names herself in the third person as the one who prepared it. **Active site.** The conditional about the color.

**Forbidden.** The result. His reply. A declaration of her love. An ingredient that keeps its color.

**Knowledge test.** She cannot know whether love is fated. Only the test will show it, and it will show in him, not in her.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's exposure: she has put herself, by name, into a test she cannot control. Transferred to a gift with no fate riding on it, the conditional is flirtation.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-ob-02 and cf-ob-03. The specimen runs cf-dt-03, null reading, in the positive: a fact that only an instrument's visible result can show.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface, where the gift carries the proposal. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (third-person self-reference), C (conditionals), D (metonymy), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-um-01 unwed mother composite

```text
handle:           Unwed Mother
rights ceiling:   in copyright (translation), Level 0, structure only
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a society that shames an unmarried mother; a father whose share of the consequence is deferred
```

**Position.** The speaker is pregnant and unmarried. The man's share of the consequence will come over a lifetime and hers is now, and the world's talk can see only her. She asks whether the listener can guess what she feels, and does not say.

**Surface.** Eight lines. A plain admission of fault. A question to the listener about her heart, left unanswered. Two lines of plant imagery for a conception out of season. The closing paradox turns her condition into a riddle: she has what she was not supposed to have, and the riddle's lightness is her refusal of the shame the world assigns. What the reader cannot know is how she feels. What the poem makes plain is that she will not be shamed.

**Active site.** The closing paradox.

**Forbidden.** Her feeling stated. The man named. Shame accepted. Pleading.

**Knowledge test.** The listener cannot know her heart, and the world knows only what shows. The poem keeps her feeling behind a question.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: exposure now, while the man's consequence waits. The attribution of individual poems to this poet is debated (P4), and no biographical reading is relied on. Transferred to a condition with no social penalty, the defiance is a pose.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-um-02 and cf-um-03.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface, in the plant imagery. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position), C (interrogation, paradox), D (metaphor), E (closure).

### cf-tb-01 two beauties composite

```text
handle:           Two Beauties
rights ceiling:   in copyright (translation), Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       a painted frame that fixes two women before they could know love
```

**Position.** The speaker looks at a painting of two sisters, beautiful and unchanging, and sees what the painting cannot give them: they will never know love. She can only question the figures and blame the painter.

**Surface.** Eight lines. A question about the sisters' ages. Their equal beauty. A projection across a hundred and then a thousand years in which they stay flat and fresh. Two questions, in figures of trees and reeds, about whether they will ever know passion or accept a dull lot. A closing question why the painter left the other pleasures out, and a verdict: gifted, but slow about love. The original is a Tang-regulated octave (P4).

**Fold.** The painting's permanence is its confinement: the figures will outlast everyone and never cross into experience, and the speaker's questions to them can be answered only by what the painter left out. The blame at the close moves the limit from the women to the man who made them. They are chaste because he did not know what to paint.

**Active site.** The closing blame of the painter.

**Forbidden.** The figures answering. A pleasure depicted. Praise of their chastity.

**Knowledge test.** The painted women cannot know love, and the speaker cannot give it to them. She can only name what the painter omitted.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is borne by the figures, fixed in beauty without experience, and the speaker's cost is her sympathy for them. Transferred to images that could never know anything, the complaint is a joke about art.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-tb-02 and cf-tb-03. The specimen runs a variant of cf-hi-02, photographer outside the frame: the maker's own limits decide what the figures can know.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface, in the tree and reed figures. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (apostrophe), C (interrogation), D (metaphor), E (ekphrastic frame, closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-gw-01 girl without a sex composite

```text
handle:           Girl without a Sex
rights ceiling:   in copyright (translation), Level 0, structure only
axis:             outward
imposed by:       a body no one can classify; a social order built on the role it cannot assign her
```

**Position.** A young woman was born, the poem says, without the sex that would place her. No one can tell what she is, the poem included, and the only certainty is social: she will never be called to the role a husband's mother calls a bride to.

**Surface.** Eight lines. A question whether the spirits who attend births quarreled and lost her part. Two images of creatures going about their business, indifferent to her. What it can know is the social result, and the close turns an anatomical mystery into a household liberation. The category that fails her also frees her from the servitude the category would have imposed.

**Active site.** The closing consolation.

**Forbidden.** A classification. Pity. Medical language. Mockery of her.

**Knowledge test.** No one, the poem included, can know what she is. The only knowable thing is what she will be spared.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost and the freedom are both hers: exclusion from the roles her society offers, including the harsh one. Readings of the poem's subject vary and are not relied on (P4). Transferred to a subject whose difference carries no social consequence, the consolation is a quip. The poem's comedy is aimed at the order, not at her, and any trial has to keep it aimed there.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-gw-02 and cf-gw-03. The specimen runs a variant of cf-ap-04, unclassified state: two kinds held level and neither chosen.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default, since a reading that drops the social order makes the poem a joke at her expense. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** C (interrogation, parallelism), D (metaphor), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-rs-01 river snail composite

```text
handle:           River Snail
rights ceiling:   in copyright (translation), Level 0, structure only
axis:             sideways
imposed by:       a decorum that forbids a woman to speak of sex; a body others will use
```

**Position.** The speaker can speak of her body and desire only as a small creature of the marsh, and inside that figure she makes the one statement she could not make plainly: a limit on what a man may do.

**Surface.** Four lines, first person. A body shaped by fate and parents into a humble creature. Its life among foul weeds, day and night. A courteous address to the man, granting entry. The original is a Tang-regulated quatrain whose double meanings rest on Vietnamese wordplay the English can only approximate (P4).

**Fold.** The creature makes the sexual subject sayable, and the poem uses that license for more than permission: it sets a boundary. Consent and refusal, which the decorum would not let her state, are stated as facts about the animal, so the refusal is deniable and exact at once. The reader hears a woman naming her terms in the one language that lets her.

**Active site.** The closing refusal.

**Forbidden.** Plain naming of the act. Breaking the figure. Coyness without the refusal. A reply.

**Knowledge test.** No one can prove what the poem means, and the speaker cannot say it in any other form. The decorum permits only the figure.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: her body offered and bounded in code, because the plain statement is forbidden. Transferred to a speaker free to state her terms, the code is a joke. Any trial must keep the adult speaker and her refusal visible; stripped of the refusal, the double entendre becomes the thing the poem resists.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-rs-02 and cf-rs-03. The file's other double-entendre poems, Jackfruit, Weaving at Night, The Paper Fan, Swinging, and Picking Flowers, run the same confinement and are held as variants. River Snail stands for the family because it sets a boundary inside the code.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface, the kernel's name for this operation. Floor-origin hypothesis, where a low creature in a low place carries the claim.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position, addressee position), D (conceit, double entendre), G (plausible deniability).
