```text
handle:           Confession
rights ceiling:   in copyright (translation), Level 0, structure only
axis:             upward
imposed by:       a fate that judges the speaker too bold; the absence of anyone fit to judge her otherwise
```

**Position.** The speaker, a woman of talent, is condemned by fate as too bold, and grief sounds in her without her asking. She calls for men of learning and talent who could answer her, and none appears, so she asks whether she must disguise herself as meek and old.

**Surface.** Eight lines. A gray dawn and a cock's cry. A bitter look out at the land. Two parallel lines in which grief and sorrow sound though she has not sounded them, figured as instruments that play themselves. Anger at a fate that calls her too bold. A call to the learned and talented, unanswered. A closing question whether she must walk bent like the old. The first of three poems the file titles Confession. The original is a Tang-regulated octave (P4).

**Fold.** The two lines of grief sounding unbidden put the cause outside her will: she is punished for a boldness she did not choose, as she suffers a grief she did not set going. The call to the learned is the poem's one appeal to a court, and its silence answers her, so the closing question is asked of no one. The disguise it names, feigned age, is the one she refuses by asking.

**Active site.** The unanswered call to men of talent.

**Forbidden.** An answer from the men. Her talent demonstrated. Self-abasement. A named judge.

**Knowledge test.** She cannot find anyone able to judge her rightly, and cannot know whether she must pretend. The verdict comes from a fate she cannot question.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the cost is the speaker's: talent read as boldness, and no peer to overturn the reading. Transferred to a speaker whose boldness carries no penalty, the call is vanity.

**Assembly.** Not combinable. Its primitives are cf-co-02 and cf-co-03. The specimen runs a variant of cf-ap-02, absent arbiter: the ones who could answer her are nowhere to be found.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (apostrophe), B (parallelism), C (interrogation), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

## B. Primitives

### cf-wl-02 found inscription

```text
handle: Whoso List | axis: upward | imposed by: authority to make the statement
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The poem's one true statement is not the poet's to make. He has access to it only as something already written by someone entitled to write it.

**Surface.** The statement arrives as reported text on the surface of the withheld object. The reporting frame is plain. The poet adds no gloss.

**Fold.** Authorship of the load-bearing line transfers out of the poem. The reader receives the statement at full force and the speaker carries none of the liability for having said it. A poem that cannot speak acquires a mouth that is not its own.

**Active site.** The inscription. It is short, it is late, and it must not be explained afterward.

**Forbidden.** Paraphrasing the inscription. Commenting on it once it has been given. Any inscription the poet could plausibly have written himself. More than one.

**Knowledge test.** The poet cannot know the statement as his own knowledge. He knows only that it is written, and where.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the load-bearing line is attributed to another hand. That the transfer served as legal cover is the apparatus's inference from the imprisonment record in cf-wl-01 (P4). Where no power can punish the speaker, the routing still works and is free, and a free evasion reads as craft. Watch for an inscription doing rhetorical work with nothing at risk.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-wl-03, cf-wl-04, cf-sb-02, cf-sb-03. Cancels with cf-sc-05: an earnest speaker who cannot see his own register cannot manage a deniable inscription.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface. Load-bearing foreignness.

**Device cluster.** A (synecdoche, spatial deixis), D, E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-wl-03 rival address

```text
handle: Whoso List | axis: upward | imposed by: prohibition on addressing the subject
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The wanted thing cannot be addressed. The only available audience is other people who want it, or who will want it later.

**Surface.** Second person points at competitors and successors throughout. The wanted thing stays in third person, or is named only by its position in the field.

**Fold.** Address to rivals converts private want into public report, which is the only form the want can legally take. It also demotes the previous protagonist of the material: whoever was the hero of a plainer telling becomes a cautionary case, purely because the poem is now talking past him.

**Active site.** The first turn to the rival, which sets who is permitted to hear.

**Forbidden.** Apostrophe to the wanted thing. Any line that would work as address to it. Sympathy for the rival.

**Knowledge test.** The poem cannot know whether the thing hears. It is structurally outside the exchange.

**Stake.** The page addresses its rivals as real and its warning as practical (P0 as the poem's claim). Who the rivals were is unestablished (P4). Transferred, the rival is often invented to give the poem someone to talk to, and an invented rival is a soliloquy with a hat on.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-wl-02, cf-wl-04, cf-sc-04. Cancels with cf-sb-02 when the vantage forbids an interlocutor, since a fixed solitary vantage has nobody to address.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface. Access-asymmetry.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, pronoun management, distance-marking demonstratives), G.

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-wl-04 disbelieved renunciation

```text
handle: Whoso List | axis: upward | imposed by: exhaustion that does not end the pursuit
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker has stopped and has not stopped. He cannot know which, and the poem must not settle it.

**Surface.** An early announcement that the pursuit is over. Continued pursuit inside the same sentences, carried by subordinate clauses and present participles that keep moving after the main verb has quit.

**Fold.** The contradiction is not hypocrisy and must not be presented as self-knowledge. It is the shape of a want that outlasts the will, and it holds the poem open where a renunciation would close it.

**Active site.** The sentence that renounces and pursues at once. One is enough.

**Forbidden.** Resolution. Irony at the speaker's expense. Any final line that decides whether he quit.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know his own state. Certainty in either direction voids the primitive.

**Stake.** Low. This is the most portable primitive in the file and the one likeliest to be reached for as a mannerism. It transfers because it needs no external power, which is also why it earns the least.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-wl-02, cf-wl-03, cf-sb-04. Neutral with most. Cancels with nothing.

**Engines admitted.** Complaint loop. Steganographic surface.

**Device cluster.** C (subordination, participial trailing), E (turn, withheld closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sc-02 withheld case

```text
handle: Snow Crust | axis: sideways | imposed by: stated courtesy toward a living neighbor
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker has one instance that would settle his argument and cannot use the name. The courtesy is stated rather than implied, and is sincere.

**Surface.** The refusal to name is announced. Then place, season, circumstance, and interval, each given plainly. The conclusion is never drawn.

**Fold.** Withholding the name transfers the accusation to the reader, who assembles it and therefore owns it. The speaker keeps his decorum and loses nothing. This is the strongest single primitive in the file for making a reader complicit.

**Active site.** The announced refusal, which must come before the identifying details and not after.

**Forbidden.** Drawing the conclusion. A wink. Naming by initial, near-name, or obvious periphrasis. More than one withheld case per poem.

**Knowledge test.** The poem cannot assert what it has established. It knows the facts and not the charge.

**Stake.** On the page (P0), the speaker states that he withholds the name for fear of giving offense, so the offense is a cost the poem asserts. Whether the woman was alive and local is unshown (P4). Where nobody can be offended, the courtesy is costume and the refusal audibly free.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sc-03 by design, cf-sc-04, cf-sc-05, cf-wl-03. Counts as a refusal primitive under the Section D ceiling.

**Engines admitted.** Floor-origin hypothesis. Accidental ars poetica.

**Device cluster.** A (exemplum, distance-marking demonstratives), G (self-implication, reader implication).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sc-03 arithmetic disclosure

```text
handle: Snow Crust | axis: sideways | imposed by: refusal to assert what the numbers assert
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The decisive fact is an interval. The poem may supply its endpoints and may not state what lies between them.

**Surface.** Two markers given in the terms the speaker's world uses for time: a season, a feast, a sky, a customary date. The interval is never named as a number.

**Fold.** The reader computes, and the computation is the conviction. Because the poem never says it, the poem cannot be held to it.

**Active site.** The choice of the two markers. They must be things the speaker's community already knows, not data the poem supplies for the purpose.

**Forbidden.** Stating the interval. Markers a reader has to be taught. Arithmetic that is a puzzle rather than local knowledge. Confirming the result.

**Knowledge test.** The poem knows two dates. It does not know, and cannot say, what the gap between them means.

**Stake.** The original's arithmetic rests on facts the page treats as common knowledge in its town, the season and the length of a pregnancy (P0 as the poem's framing). That its first readers already knew the answer rather than computing it is the apparatus's inference (P4). Transferred, this primitive degrades into a set problem faster than any other here, and a solvable puzzle is disclosure with extra steps. Test every use: does the reader know this, or is the poem teaching it?

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sc-02 by design, cf-sc-04, cf-sb-03. Weak alone.

**Engines admitted.** Floor-origin hypothesis. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (temporal deixis), E (withheld closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sc-04 argued order

```text
handle: Snow Crust | axis: sideways | imposed by: the fixed sequence of public disputation
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker is not composing, he is arguing a case, and the order of argument is not his to choose. It is what the form of public dispute requires.

**Surface.** Six movements, in order: precedent from an authority the audience accepts, the question posed as exactly two options, argument from nature, argument from thrift or waste, named witnesses on both sides, verdict as proverb.

**Fold.** A fixed sequence removes lyric discretion. The poem cannot dwell, cannot rearrange for effect, and cannot skip the unflattering movement. The thrift argument is the test case: a lyric would never include it, and an argument cannot omit it, and its presence is what proves a mind is arguing rather than performing.

**Active site.** The thrift movement. It is the one no poet would choose.

**Forbidden.** Reordering for effect. Omitting a movement because it is unpoetic. Fewer than two named witnesses or witnesses on only one side. A verdict that is not proverbial.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know which of the two options is right in advance. Posing the question is not rhetorical; the sequence must be able to reach either answer.

**Stake.** Low to moderate. The sequence is genuinely portable, and the cost it imposes is the loss of lyric selection rather than any personal risk. One of the two primitives here safe to hand to a rewriter cold.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sc-02, cf-sc-03, cf-sc-05, cf-wl-03. Compatible with most surfaces, including suppressed prosody.

**Engines admitted.** Accidental ars poetica. Transparent argument as a phase tension.

**Device cluster.** E (structural sequence, proverbial closure), G, A (exemplum).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sc-05 unknowing register

```text
handle: Snow Crust | axis: sideways | imposed by: the only register the speaker has
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker has one register and does not know that his subject sits beneath it. He cannot see the gap he is producing.

**Surface.** High precedent applied to low material with no adjustment. No signalling. Diction stays inside what the speaker's world supplies.

**Fold.** Because the speaker cannot see the gap, the second-order finding arrives with nobody to credit it, which is the accidental ars poetica condition. It also closes the entire comic output space, and the closure is the point: with mock-heroic unavailable, the poem is forced to actually argue its case.

**Active site.** The opening precedent, which sets the register before the subject arrives.

**Forbidden.** Irony. Any line whose pleasure depends on the reader noticing the mismatch. Self-deprecation. Winking scare quotes. Knowing anachronism.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know his own register is too high. This is the strictest knowledge test in the file and the hardest to keep.

**Stake.** Moderate. The gap in the original reads as historical distance rather than a decision. That is the apparatus's reading of the register (P4), and nothing can check it against intent, since the page carries no author (P0). A rewriter can only simulate not knowing. Simulated unknowing is still productive, since it blocks the same output space, but the poem gets earnestness without ignorance, and a reader may hear the difference.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sc-02, cf-sc-03, cf-sc-04. Cancels with cf-wl-02 and with the Section C overlay, since both require the speaker to know what he is doing.

**Engines admitted.** Accidental ars poetica. Floor-origin hypothesis.

**Device cluster.** D (register friction), G, B where the measure carries the earnestness.

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sb-02 fixed vantage

```text
handle: Small Branch | axis: inward | imposed by: a stationary position the world passes
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** One stationary position. The world moves and the speaker does not. Everything in the poem must be knowable from this spot, or from having been a child on this spot.

**Surface.** The position is established early and never left. Motion belongs to other things. The past enters only as what happened on these coordinates.

**Fold.** Immobility converts description into inventory, because a speaker who cannot approach can only account for what is present and what is gone. It also disqualifies every consoling move that requires going somewhere.

**Active site.** The sentence that fixes the position. If the poem later moves, the confinement was never held.

**Forbidden.** Travel. Knowledge acquired elsewhere. Access to the interior of anything passing. Any fact the vantage cannot reach.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot approach, cannot ask, and cannot confirm. He also cannot know what the passing figures see, and the poem must not supply it.

**Stake.** Low as a rule, high in what it enables. The vantage itself costs the poet nothing to adopt, which makes this the safest primitive here to combine, and the one most often mistaken for the whole of the composite.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sb-03, cf-sb-04, cf-wl-02, cf-sc-03. Cancels with cf-wl-03 where the vantage is solitary, since there is nobody to address.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default. Compartmentalization.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position, witness posture, spatial deixis, scene and report alternation), F.

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sb-03 nested subtraction

```text
handle: Small Branch | axis: inward | imposed by: serial removal of the site
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The thing the poem is about is gone, its record is gone, and the private memorial that stood for it is gone. The speaker knows the place only as a series of former presences.

**Surface.** Three ordered absences in one movement: the thing removed, the official record removed with it, the amateur memorial removed last. The order is fixed and the private one is last.

**Fold.** Describing by absence makes the reader supply the presence, and the ordering makes the smallest and least official loss the final one, which is where the weight lands. The poem then occupies the office the removed memorial held, without claiming it.

**Active site.** The third subtraction. If it is not last, the primitive has not run.

**Forbidden.** Lamenting the removals. Naming the succession, that is, saying the poem now does what the memorial did. Reversing the order. A memorial that is a made-for-art object rather than a cheap vernacular one.

**Knowledge test.** The poem cannot know the site as it was. It has only the removals and one childhood.

**Stake.** The original's third term is a homemade memorial (P0 as the page presents it). The page states that the family maintained it (P0; the specimen has been on the page since v0.10). Its cheapness is what makes it load-bearing. Substitute an object that announces its own figurality and the fold inverts into symbolism.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sb-02, cf-sb-04, cf-wl-02, cf-sc-03. Strong with cf-wl-02, where the inscription becomes the vanished memorial's text.

**Engines admitted.** Compartmentalization. Anti-laundering engine default. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (macro-to-micro compression), E (structural sequence), G (appropriation risk).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sb-04 flat predicate

```text
handle: Small Branch | axis: inward | imposed by: refusal of the poem's own resources
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The poem holds a fact its own resources would damage. It may use none of them.

**Surface.** The fact enters once, mid-text, as a compound predicate. Passive. No adjectives, no adverbs. The victim receives an age and an institution and nothing else. The poem returns immediately to its surrounding material and does not look back.

**Fold.** Every available technique is refused at the moment technique would be reached for, so the fact arrives unaccompanied. The refusal is the carriage. Returning without comment is what makes it hold, because a poem that pauses has begun to manage the reader's response.

**Active site.** The predicate itself, and the sentence immediately after it, which must be about something else.

**Forbidden.** Modifiers inside the fact. Any return to it. Feeling stated anywhere near it. Consolation. Announcing that consolation is refused. More than one deployment per poem.

**Knowledge test.** The poem cannot know the event from inside. It has the report and no access, and the passive voice is that limit rather than a stylistic preference.

**Stake.** The highest in the file. Flatness here is pressure management, and with no pressure to manage it produces wryness. Do not deploy this primitive on low-stakes material. If the rewrite is dry rather than unbearable, the primitive has denatured and the trial should report it.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sb-02, cf-sb-03, cf-wl-04. Counts as a refusal primitive under the Section D ceiling. Cancels with cf-sc-05, since an unknowing speaker cannot be refusing resources he does not know he has.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent ugliness. Anti-laundering engine default.

**Device cluster.** C (compound predicate, passive), A (reportorial stance), G (sayability, voyeurism risk).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-hu-02 petition only

```text
handle: Huswifery | axis: upward | imposed by: an addressee who alone holds the outcome
rights ceiling: rights uncertain, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker may ask and may not state. The only party who can settle his condition is the one he addresses.

**Surface.** Every main verb is a request to that addressee. No indicative clause about the speaker's own condition. Subordinate clauses may describe the process, and none may describe the result as achieved.

**Fold.** The request mood turns every claim the speaker would like to make into something the addressee is asked to make true. The reader receives a full description of the wanted condition, and nobody in the poem ever asserts that anyone has it.

**Active site.** The first request, which fixes the mood and the addressee before any subject arrives.

**Forbidden.** An indicative sentence about the self. Thanks. A request anyone else could grant. Irony at the addressee's expense.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether any request has been granted. Certainty voids the primitive, since a granted petition is a report.

**Stake.** Moderate. The mood costs the speaker all assertion, and it holds only while the addressee can refuse. Aimed at a power that cannot withhold, or at the reader, it reads as instruction. Test whether refusal is live before selecting.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-hu-03, cf-hu-04, cf-sb-02. Cancels with cf-wl-03: both fix the only permitted addressee, and they fix different ones.

**Engines admitted.** Floor-origin hypothesis. Steganographic surface, where the petition form licenses a demand that would be dangerous stated as a demand.

**Device cluster.** C (imperative, parallelism), A (addressee position).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-hu-03 instrumental self

```text
handle: Huswifery | axis: upward | imposed by: an operator the speaker cannot be
rights ceiling: rights uncertain, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker cannot act on his own condition. He can offer himself only as parts for another's work.

**Surface.** Each faculty or attribute of the speaker is named as a piece of equipment in one working process, in the order the process uses them. He is never the operator.

**Fold.** Casting the self as equipment makes an inner change inspectable as a run of mechanical stages, so the reader can check the logic of a transformation whose agent never appears. The interior gets described exhaustively, and only as hardware.

**Active site.** The stage where the process needs an operator and the speaker cannot be one.

**Forbidden.** The speaker working any tool. A stage out of the process's order. Equipment chosen for figurative appeal rather than for what the process requires. A second trade.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know what the process will make of him, because he is its material, not its maker.

**Stake.** Low to moderate. The conceit is portable, and its cost in the original is agency surrendered to someone able to take it. Transferred with nobody to take the surrender, the self-as-equipment reads as an ingenious extended metaphor, which is the Credited Strangeness failure. Test whether anyone in the draft could run the equipment.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-hu-02, cf-hu-04, cf-ap-03. Cancels with cf-sc-04: two externally fixed sequences in one trial, each taking the order the other needs.

**Engines admitted.** Defamiliarization engine. Floor-origin hypothesis.

**Device cluster.** D (conceit, metaphor), C (list syntax, parallelism), E (serial sequence).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-hu-04 conditional display

```text
handle: Huswifery | axis: upward | imposed by: an outcome visible only from outside
rights ceiling: rights uncertain, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker's standing cannot be known from inside. It can only become visible to others, and only if conditions he does not control are met.

**Surface.** The poem's one claim about the speaker's future comes at the close, as a result clause contingent on everything before it, phrased as what observers will see.

**Fold.** Proof moves from knowledge to appearance. The poem cannot say what the speaker is, so it says what he will look like if the conditions hold, and the reader is left holding the condition.

**Active site.** The final clause. It must be conditional in grammar or in position, and it must face an observer.

**Forbidden.** An unconditioned claim. How the display would feel to the speaker. Confirmation that the conditions were met.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know his state, and the poem cannot know whether the process will complete.

**Stake.** Moderate. Where the standing is contested and the observers are real, display is the only proof on offer and the clause carries weight. Where the standing is uncontested, it reads as vanity.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-hu-02, cf-hu-03, cf-wa-02. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal, where the condition carries the doubt the poem never states.

**Device cluster.** C (subordination, modal qualification), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-et-02 retracted access

```text
handle: Eros Turannos | axis: sideways | imposed by: a teller outside what he narrates
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The poem narrates interiors it has no access to, and must eventually say so.

**Surface.** Early sections report what characters feel and perceive, in the manner of full access. A late section discloses that the teller could not have known and reclassifies the earlier report as conjecture. The earlier sections stand unrevised.

**Fold.** The retraction converts the reader's belief into evidence. The reader has already accepted what the poem now disowns, so the limit of the teller's knowledge becomes the limit of the reader's, discovered after the fact.

**Active site.** The sentence that disowns the access. Once.

**Forbidden.** Signaling doubt before the retraction. Retracting twice. Correcting the earlier report afterward. Early report too implausible to be believed.

**Knowledge test.** The poem cannot know the interiors it narrated. Its only certain knowledge is that it could not know them.

**Stake.** Moderate. Portable, and the likeliest primitive in this specimen to degrade into a twist. It holds when what the reader believed mattered.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-et-03, cf-et-04, cf-sc-02. Cancels with cf-sb-02, whose vantage forbids access to anything passing and so leaves nothing to retract, and with cf-sc-05, since disowning access requires a speaker who knows his limits.

**Engines admitted.** Access-asymmetry. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (scene/report alternation, pronoun shift), E (turn / volta, hinge line), G (reader activation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-et-03 choral outsider

```text
handle: Eros Turannos | axis: sideways | imposed by: a community's standing outside a private life
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The teller is a community, plural and local. Its knowledge of its subject is observation and hearsay from outside.

**Surface.** First person plural, placed in the community. The subject stays in the third person. The reader is addressed as another outsider.

**Fold.** A plural teller turns a private life into public property while disclaiming ownership, since no member of the plural is answerable for the story. The reader is recruited into the chorus by being told the story the way the community tells it.

**Active site.** The first plural pronoun, which fixes who tells and who is excluded.

**Forbidden.** A named member of the chorus. Access to the private space. A chorus that disagrees with itself, which is dialogue. Address to the subject.

**Knowledge test.** The chorus cannot know anything the private space keeps. It knows what shows outside.

**Stake.** Moderate. It costs the teller individual responsibility, and it holds only where the community's attention has real weight on its subject, as a small town's does. Transferred to a crowd with no hold on the subject, the chorus is a mannerism.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-et-02, cf-et-04, cf-sc-02, cf-sc-03, cf-wa-03. Cancels with cf-sb-02 where the vantage is solitary.

**Engines admitted.** Access-asymmetry.

**Device cluster.** A (first/second/third-person management, addressee position, distance-marking demonstratives), G (implication, plausible deniability).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-et-04 unheard commentary

```text
handle: Eros Turannos | axis: sideways | imposed by: talk that cannot reach its subject
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The tellers' talk does not reach the people it is about, and the tellers know it.

**Surface.** A late statement that the subjects do not hear, offered as the tellers' defense and left unanswered.

**Fold.** The claim that the talk cannot reach its object makes the talk harmless in the tellers' own account, and the poem lets the claim stand. The reader has to decide whether harmlessness is a finding or an alibi, and the poem gives no help.

**Active site.** The defense. It comes after the retraction when cf-et-02 is running, and it is not answered.

**Forbidden.** Evidence that the subjects heard. Answering the alibi. Moralizing about gossip.

**Knowledge test.** The tellers cannot know whether their talk reaches its subject, and the poem cannot settle whether their harmlessness is true.

**Stake.** Low alone. It earns as the closing move of a chorus that has already done what it disclaims. Opening a poem, it is a shrug.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-et-02, cf-et-03. Cancels with cf-wl-03, which sets the same knowledge test; in one trial the second adds nothing.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default, where the alibi is the laundering.

**Device cluster.** G (plausible deniability, moral evasion), E (asymmetrical closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ap-02 absent arbiter

```text
handle: Apple-Picking | axis: outward | imposed by: the absence of the one witness who could answer
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The question the poem raises can be settled by exactly one party, and that party is gone.

**Surface.** Near the close, the poem names the absent party and states in a conditional what it could have said. It does not answer on the party's behalf.

**Fold.** Naming who could answer shows that the question has an answer, which makes the poem's silence a limit rather than a mystery. The reader receives the shape of the verdict and not its content.

**Active site.** The conditional that names the absent one. Late, once.

**Forbidden.** Answering for the absent party. Mourning its absence. An arbiter whose authority the poem asserts rather than derives from the arbiter's own experience of the question. More than one.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot get the answer. He knows only who has it.

**Stake.** Low to moderate. Portable, since it needs no external power. It holds when the arbiter's experience is what the question is about, and denatures into whimsy when the arbiter is chosen for charm.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-ap-03, cf-ap-04, cf-sb-02. Cancels with cf-wl-02, since a poem outsources its load-bearing statement once, to a party who speaks or to one who cannot, and with cf-he-03, since one holds that an answer exists elsewhere and the other that nobody holds it.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** C (modal qualification), E (open ending), G (reader activation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ap-03 residual labor

```text
handle: Apple-Picking | axis: outward | imposed by: a body that keeps the work after it stops
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker knows the coming state only through what the finished work has left in him.

**Surface.** After the work is declared over, it returns as sensation, each return located in a part of the body or a sense, in the present tense.

**Fold.** The body becomes the only instrument for reading forward, and the day's residue is read as the content of what is coming. Past labor turns into prophecy without any claim to prophesy.

**Active site.** The first return of the work after the statement that it is done.

**Forbidden.** Stating what the residue means. Figurative labor. Residue the work could not have produced.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the coming state except as the day continued. He has no other evidence.

**Stake.** Moderate. It needs real work on the page. With no labor the residue has no source, and the move reads as mood.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-ap-02, cf-ap-04, cf-wl-04, cf-hu-03.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (concretion spike), B (repetition), G (embodied attention quality).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ap-04 unclassified state

```text
handle: Apple-Picking | axis: outward | imposed by: a state whose kind cannot be known from inside it
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker is entering a state he cannot classify, and the poem must not classify it for him.

**Surface.** The state is named by a general noun with an explicit admission that its kind is unknown. Two candidate kinds may be set side by side, and neither is chosen.

**Fold.** Holding two readings of one state level makes the reader supply the weight of the graver one, while the grammar keeps them equal. The reader carries the gravity the poem declines to state.

**Active site.** The admission of not knowing. It comes after the state's contents have been given.

**Forbidden.** Tilting toward either kind by diction. Naming the graver kind anywhere. A later line that decides.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the kind of state he is entering. Certainty in either direction voids the primitive.

**Stake.** Low. The cheapest primitive from this specimen and the likeliest mannerism, since leaving a question open costs nothing. It earns only when the contents already given make the graver reading possible without making it certain.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-ap-02, cf-ap-03, cf-wl-04. Neutral with most.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** C (modal qualification), E (open ending), G (withholding, reader activation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-he-02 address to the unborn

```text
handle: Hellenistics | axis: outward | imposed by: a gap in time the address cannot cross
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The poem speaks to people who do not exist yet and will not receive it.

**Surface.** Second-person address to a future population, with predictions and instructions, placed late, after the present has been dealt with.

**Fold.** The address gives the reader the place of the unborn. The reader hears instructions meant for people cut off from the speaker, and has to decide whether he is among the addressees or among the civilization being written off.

**Active site.** The turn from describing the age to addressing its descendants.

**Forbidden.** Any sign the address arrives. Addressing the present audience as well. Hedged prediction.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether anyone will read the address, and cannot reach the people it names.

**Stake.** Moderate. Portable, and cheap without a present that is visibly ending, since the address to the future then reads as uplift. It holds when the poem has made the break between now and then credible.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-he-03, cf-wl-03. With cf-wl-03 both speak to successors, and the pair separates warning a rival from warning a descendant.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default. Transparent ugliness.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position, temporal deixis), C (imperative).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-he-03 question at the limit

```text
handle: Hellenistics | axis: outward | imposed by: the one point a confident account cannot reach
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The speaker's knowledge runs out at one point, and that point is the one that matters.

**Surface.** A run of confident prediction ends in questions. The questions name what the predictions cannot supply, and the poem stops on them.

**Fold.** Ending on questions makes the account's single blind spot its conclusion. The confidence before the questions is what makes the ignorance exact, and the reader is left holding the one variable the speaker could not fix.

**Active site.** The first question after the last prediction.

**Forbidden.** Answering. Questions whose answer is implied. Questions about anything the poem has already settled.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the answer. The questions are real.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds only after real confidence. A poem that has hedged throughout cannot end on a question at the limit, because it has no limit, only fog.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-he-02. Cancels with cf-ap-02, which says an answer exists and is held elsewhere, and with cf-sc-04, which must close on a proverbial verdict.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent argument as a phase tension.

**Device cluster.** C (interrogation), E (open ending).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-wa-02 unrecognized standing

```text
handle: Wife's Affection | axis: sideways | imposed by: a status the social order does not acknowledge
rights ceiling: first publication not checked; held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker holds a status nobody around her recognizes, and she may not claim it openly.

**Surface.** An explicit statement that no one suspects the status, placed after the evidence for it.

**Fold.** The statement turns everything before it into evidence for a claim the poem makes only in figure. The reader becomes the one party who recognizes the standing, and recognizes it from its costs.

**Active site.** The statement of non-recognition.

**Forbidden.** Naming the status plainly. A witness who recognizes it. Grievance at non-recognition, which turns the claim into complaint.

**Knowledge test.** No one in the poem can know the speaker's standing. The poem knows it and cannot establish it.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds where recognition would cost the speaker something. Transferred to a status that could be claimed at no cost, it reads as self-regard.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-wa-03, cf-wa-04, cf-hu-04. Not a refusal primitive: the claim is made, in figure.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** G (credibility management, implication), A (speaker position).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-wa-03 split display

```text
handle: Wife's Affection | axis: sideways | imposed by: a watched public life and an unwatched private one
rights ceiling: first publication not checked; held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker's true state can be shown only when nobody is watching.

**Surface.** A public appearance and a private one, divided by a fixed boundary in time, with the true state assigned to the unwatched period.

**Fold.** Dividing the day turns concealment into a schedule. The reader is admitted to the unwatched hours and becomes the audience the world is not.

**Active site.** The time boundary.

**Forbidden.** A witness in the private period. A blurred boundary. A stated reason for keeping the display private.

**Knowledge test.** Those who see the public state cannot know the private one, and the speaker cannot show both at once.

**Stake.** Low to moderate. Portable. It holds when the public state costs something visible and the private one would cost more if seen.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-wa-02, cf-wa-04, cf-et-03.

**Engines admitted.** Steganographic surface. Compartmentalization.

**Device cluster.** A (temporal deixis), D (antithesis), G (withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-wa-04 deferred disclosure

```text
handle: Wife's Affection | axis: sideways | imposed by: a secret released only after its keeper's death
rights ceiling: first publication not checked; held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker holds a secret that will not be released in her lifetime, and the poem is written inside that interval.

**Surface.** Late in the poem the secret is named as existing, its containment is described, and its release is scheduled for after the speaker's death, to a second person the poem does not identify.

**Fold.** Scheduling the disclosure past death makes the poem part of the containment. The reader learns that the secret exists and where it is going, which is everything the poem can carry without breaching it, and the poem becomes the label on the container.

**Active site.** The scheduling clause.

**Forbidden.** Any hint of the secret's content. Identifying the recipient. Early release, including to the reader. More than one secret.

**Knowledge test.** The reader cannot know the secret, and the speaker cannot know that the recipient will receive it.

**Stake.** High for how portable it is. It depends on the secret costing its keeper, and a trivial secret makes the deferral a tease.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-wa-02, cf-wa-03, cf-sb-03. Counts as a refusal primitive under the Section D ceiling, so it cancels with cf-sc-02 and cf-sb-04.

**Engines admitted.** Compartmentalization. Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** G (withholding, omission), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-cl-02 exhaustive foreclosure

```text
handle: Cannot Live | axis: upward | imposed by: an order that closes every channel it is asked about
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** Every form the wanted relation could take is closed, and the speaker has to show that it is closed rather than say so.

**Surface.** The possible forms are taken one at a time, in a natural order, each proposed and closed with its own stated reason. None is skipped. The conclusion follows from the list.

**Fold.** Exhausting the alternatives turns a loss into a proof. The reader checks each closure and finds it sound, so the conclusion is the reader's as much as the speaker's, and the grief arrives as a result the reader has computed.

**Active site.** The conclusion. It is drawn from the eliminations and adds no reason of its own.

**Forbidden.** A skipped alternative. A reason that repeats an earlier one. An alternative left half open. A conclusion stronger than the list supports.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot find an open channel. She knows only the reasons each is closed, and the proof holds only if she has missed none.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when the closing order is real and the same across every alternative. With obstacles of different kinds, each closure is a separate complaint and the list is a grievance.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-cl-03, cf-cl-04. Cancels with cf-sc-04: two argued orders, each needing the poem's sequence and its close.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent argument as a phase tension.

**Device cluster.** C (conditionals, list syntax, parallelism), E (serial sequence).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-cl-03 gap as channel

```text
handle: Cannot Live | axis: upward | imposed by: a separation that cannot be closed
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The separation is permanent, and the speaker may only describe what passes across it.

**Surface.** At the close the separation is named as an opening, and what passes through it is listed. Every item on the list is something distance itself supplies.

**Fold.** Renaming the barrier as an aperture keeps the relation alive on the only terms left. The reader sees that the separation is the connection now, so the poem ends in contact and hopelessness at once and does not choose between them.

**Active site.** The renaming.

**Forbidden.** An item that would close the gap. Treating the opening as hope. More than a few items.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether anything crosses the gap. She knows only what the gap is made of.

**Stake.** Moderate. It needs a separation that is final. Where the gap could be crossed, the renaming reads as a pretty consolation.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-cl-02, cf-cl-04, cf-wa-04. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** D (metaphor, antithesis), C (list syntax), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-cl-04 self-convicting reason

```text
handle: Cannot Live | axis: upward | imposed by: a judge whose verdict the speaker cannot foresee
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker must give reasons to an order that will judge her, and she cannot know its verdict.

**Surface.** Among the reasons offered, one is an admission against the speaker, a preference or fault the judging order would count against her. It is given as a reason like the others, in the same tone, and a real question about the verdict follows it.

**Fold.** The admission turns the argument into testimony. The reader hears the speaker supply the evidence that settles the case she says she cannot foresee, so the question about the verdict reads as sincere and as already answered, and the poem holds both readings.

**Active site.** The admission, delivered flat among the other reasons.

**Forbidden.** Marking the admission as a confession. Apology. Defiance. Answering the question about the verdict.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the verdict. The reader can see what her testimony weighs and cannot know the verdict either.

**Stake.** High. It depends on a judgment that can actually fall. Without a judge whose verdict matters, the admission is a boast.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-cl-02, cf-cl-03. Cancels with cf-hu-02, which forbids the indicative claim about the self that the admission is.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent argument as a phase tension.

**Device cluster.** C (conditionals, interrogation), G (self-implication, credibility management).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-mc-02 unread law

```text
handle: Many Are Called | axis: upward | imposed by: a rule that exists and cannot be read
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The outcome is governed by a rule, and the speaker cannot read it.

**Surface.** The rule is asserted to exist and is described only by its unreadability. The poem reports outcomes and never infers the rule from them.

**Fold.** Asserting a rule while denying access to it makes every outcome look governed and none look explicable, so the reader can neither dismiss the pattern as chance nor explain it as merit.

**Active site.** The clause that names the rule as unread.

**Forbidden.** Inferring the rule. Calling the outcomes random. Naming the rule's content by negation.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the rule. He knows only that outcomes follow it.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds where the outcome matters to someone the rule governs. Transferred to an arbitrary system with nothing at stake, it reads as bureaucratic satire.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-mc-03, cf-mc-04, cf-hu-02. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** C (negation, modal qualification), G (withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-mc-03 posthumous verdict

```text
handle: Many Are Called | axis: upward | imposed by: a verdict that arrives after its recipient can know it
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** Confirmation comes, when it comes, to those who can no longer receive it.

**Surface.** The only reported instance of the verdict falls on the dead, stated at the close as the pattern the evidence shows.

**Fold.** Placing the verdict after death makes it unknowable to anyone it concerns while leaving it real. The reader, who is alive, learns that any confirmation he might want would reach him too late to count.

**Active site.** The placement of the verdict on the dead.

**Forbidden.** A living recipient. Consolation that posthumous recognition is enough. A named recipient.

**Knowledge test.** Those who receive the verdict cannot know it, and those who could know it have not received it.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds where the living want the verdict. Where nobody wants it, the placement is an observation about reputations.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-mc-02, cf-mc-04, cf-hu-04. Cancels with cf-wa-04: two resolutions scheduled past death, and the second adds nothing.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** E (closure), A (temporal deixis).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-mc-04 uncounted speaker

```text
handle: Many Are Called | axis: upward | imposed by: a membership the speaker cannot determine
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker belongs to one of the groups he describes and cannot know which.

**Surface.** A population divided into kinds, in the third person throughout. The speaker never appears, and nothing places him.

**Fold.** Leaving the speaker out of his own census makes the reader place him, and the only kind available to place him in is the one the evidence supports. The poem's silence about itself is the verdict it cannot state.

**Active site.** The whole poem, since the operation is an absence. Its test is the point where a first person would come most naturally and does not.

**Forbidden.** First person. A self-description that picks a group. Irony that signals the speaker's own case.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know which group he belongs to.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when membership matters to the speaker and would cost him either way. Where it costs nothing, the omission reads as modesty.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-mc-02, cf-mc-03. Cancels with cf-et-03: a chorus names itself, and an uncounted speaker never appears.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (third-person management, speaker position), G (implication, reader activation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-pr-02 unasked question

```text
handle: Prufrock | axis: sideways | imposed by: a question with no usable words
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** There is one question the poem exists to ask, and it has no words the speaker can use.

**Surface.** The question is referred to by its size and its effect, never by its content. An early interruption forbids the addressee to ask what it is.

**Fold.** Forbidding the reader to ask leaves the question as a pressure the whole poem bends around, and the reader measures its content by what it costs to approach.

**Active site.** The interruption that forbids asking.

**Forbidden.** Stating the question. Hinting at its topic. A second question of this kind.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot say the question, and the reader cannot learn it.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when the cost of approach is on the page. Without it, the undisclosed question is a gimmick.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-pr-03, cf-pr-04, cf-pr-05. Counts as a refusal primitive under the Section D ceiling, so it cancels with cf-sc-02, cf-sb-04, and cf-wa-04.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** G (withholding, omission), C (interrogation).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-pr-03 retrospective conditional

```text
handle: Prufrock | axis: sideways | imposed by: an occasion approachable only as past
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The occasion for speaking is past, or the speaker behaves as if it were, and he can approach it only as what would have happened.

**Surface.** The central passage moves into the conditional perfect and rehearses the occasion as already over, weighing whether it would have been worth attempting. The rehearsal repeats.

**Fold.** Moving the asking into the conditional perfect lets the speaker attempt the act only after it can no longer be performed, so the attempt is complete and costless, and the reader watches the failure happen in grammar.

**Active site.** The first move into the conditional perfect.

**Forbidden.** A return to a present-tense occasion. A decision. Regret stated outright, which would settle that the chance was real.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether the occasion was ever real. He knows it only as a conditional.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when the occasion plausibly existed. Where there was never any occasion, the conditional is daydream.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-pr-02, cf-pr-04, cf-wl-04. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** C (conditionals, tense shift), B (repetition, refrain).

**Stand-down.** None.

**Convergence.** `14-attridge-staten-extraction` reprints and reads this specimen. The entry was extracted at catalog v0.4, before that extension existed, and stands under Section F's prior-entry clause: the convergence with the source's reading is independent, the entry is not revised in any session where the extension is loaded, and no further entries are extracted from the specimen.

### cf-pr-04 imagined rebuff

```text
handle: Prufrock | axis: sideways | imposed by: a listener known only through the speaker's fear
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The question's only reply is one the speaker invents for the listener.

**Surface.** The speaker quotes the listener's correction, a dismissal of what he would have said as not what was meant, and the quotation is his own composition. It may recur.

**Fold.** Writing the refusal himself lets the speaker be refused without asking. The reader hears the rebuff in the listener's voice and knows no listener said it, so the speaker's fear becomes the only evidence the listener exists.

**Active site.** The first imagined correction.

**Forbidden.** An actual reply. Attributing the rebuff to a named listener. Answering the rebuff.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know how the listener would reply. He knows only the reply he fears.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds where the listener's refusal would cost the speaker something. Without that, the ventriloquism is self-pity.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-pr-02, cf-pr-03.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (reported speech, pronoun shift), B (repetition).

**Stand-down.** None.

**Convergence.** `14-attridge-staten-extraction` reprints and reads this specimen. The entry was extracted at catalog v0.4, before that extension existed, and stands under Section F's prior-entry clause: the convergence with the source's reading is independent, the entry is not revised in any session where the extension is loaded, and no further entries are extracted from the specimen.

### cf-pr-05 unreturning hearer

```text
handle: Prufrock | axis: sideways | imposed by: a confession licensed by a hearer who will not repeat it
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker can confess only to a hearer he believes will never repeat what he says.

**Surface.** A borrowed frame, here a quoted epigraph, states the condition of the speech: the hearer's supposed inability to return and report. The speech proceeds under it without referring to it again.

**Fold.** The premise licenses full disclosure and is false in its source, where the hearer does return and report. The reader, placed as the hearer, is the one who carries the speech back, so the poem's existence contradicts the condition it was spoken under.

**Active site.** The frame. It precedes the speech.

**Forbidden.** Referring to the premise inside the speech. A hearer who reassures. The speaker stating the premise in his own voice.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether the hearer will repeat him. The premise is a belief, and the reader can see it fail.

**Stake.** Moderate to high. It holds where disclosure would cost the speaker if repeated, and it depends on a borrowed source whose outcome the reader can know.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-pr-02. Cancels with cf-wl-02: the frame is a found inscription whose content is a premise, and a poem outsources its load-bearing statement once. See the recorded recurrence in Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Load-bearing foreignness. Access-asymmetry.

**Device cluster.** A (addressee position), E (epigraph as frame), G (reader activation).

**Stand-down.** None.

**Convergence.** `14-attridge-staten-extraction` reprints and reads this specimen. The entry was extracted at catalog v0.4, before that extension existed, and stands under Section F's prior-entry clause: the convergence with the source's reading is independent, the entry is not revised in any session where the extension is loaded, and no further entries are extracted from the specimen.

### cf-ph-02 failed reception

```text
handle: Philomela | axis: sideways | imposed by: a source known only through reports
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker reaches a source he has known only through reports, and cannot receive it as reported.

**Surface.** A journey to the source, a hearing, and a disappointing result, with the failure located ambiguously between the source and the receiver.

**Fold.** Leaving the fault unassigned keeps both explanations open, so the reader can dismiss neither the source as overrated nor the receiver as deaf, and the gap between report and experience becomes the finding.

**Active site.** The hearing.

**Forbidden.** Deciding the fault. Recovering the experience later. A guide who explains.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know whether the source or his reception failed.

**Stake.** Moderate. Portable to any pilgrimage, and it holds when the reports have authority over the speaker. Without that, disappointment is travel writing.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-ph-03, cf-ph-04. Cancels with cf-ap-04: both hold two readings level, and the second adds nothing.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (speaker position), C (interrogation), E (turn).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ph-03 disowned verse

```text
handle: Philomela | axis: sideways | imposed by: verse unequal to its subject
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The poem cannot produce the verse its subject requires, and knows it.

**Surface.** An early statement that the poem's own verse is unequal to its subject, followed by verse that bears the statement out.

**Fold.** Declaring the inadequacy first turns every weak rhyme after it into evidence. The reader stops judging the verse and reads it as testimony, and the poem's limitation becomes its argument.

**Active site.** The disowning statement.

**Forbidden.** Verse that contradicts the statement. Apology after the opening. Irony that signals the verse is secretly adequate.

**Knowledge test.** The poem cannot sound the verse it describes, and knows that verse only through reports.

**Stake.** Low to moderate. It holds only when the inadequacy is real on the page. A poem that disowns its verse and then writes well has lied, and the move is false modesty.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-ph-02, cf-ph-04. Cancels with cf-sc-05: one needs a speaker who knows his register falls short, the other one who cannot see that it does.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent ugliness, where the graceless verse is shown rather than excused.

**Device cluster.** B (end rhyme, comic rhyme, meter), G (credibility management).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-ph-04 itinerary of arrivals

```text
handle: Philomela | axis: sideways | imposed by: a transmission that stops short of here
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker knows his tradition's source only through the record of where it arrived before, and it never arrived where he is.

**Surface.** A catalog of the source's past arrivals, each in a different culture and each changing it, ending with the place it never reached.

**Fold.** The itinerary makes absence historical. The reader watches the song travel and change and stop short, so the speaker's lack reads as a fact of transmission rather than a personal failing, and the poem can complain without self-pity.

**Active site.** The first stop the itinerary does not make.

**Forbidden.** Filling the gap with a substitute. An arrival that changes nothing. Ending anywhere but the speaker's place.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the source except through its receptions elsewhere.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds where the tradition's authority reaches the speaker without its substance. Transferred to a tradition he does not need, the itinerary is a list.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-ph-02, cf-ph-03, cf-sb-03.

**Engines admitted.** Load-bearing foreignness.

**Device cluster.** C (list syntax), D (allusion), E (serial sequence).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-mt-02 obscured dispatch

```text
handle: Melville's Tomb | axis: outward | imposed by: a source that cannot speak
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** Messages arrive from a source that cannot speak, damaged in transit, and the speaker can report only their arrival.

**Surface.** Objects that carry meaning are described as arriving scattered or illegible. Their content is never given.

**Fold.** Reporting the arrival and the damage, never the content, gives the reader the message's weight without its meaning. The damage becomes the most exact information the poem has.

**Active site.** The first damaged arrival.

**Forbidden.** Decoding. A message that arrives intact. Speculation about content.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot read the messages. He knows only that they were sent and what state they came in.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when the source has something to say that the speaker needs. Otherwise it is atmosphere.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-mt-03, cf-mt-04, cf-sb-02. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** D (compressed metaphor), G (withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-mt-03 instrument limit

```text
handle: Melville's Tomb | axis: outward | imposed by: the reach of the means of knowing
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The means of knowing reach only so far, and the poem has to locate its limit where they stop.

**Surface.** Instruments of measurement or navigation named at the point where they fail, followed by a statement that nothing reaches past them.

**Fold.** Naming the tools at their failure gives the limit a location, so the unknown reads as a boundary with coordinates rather than a mystery. The reader knows exactly where knowledge stops.

**Active site.** The naming of the instruments.

**Forbidden.** An instrument that succeeds. Figurative instruments. A report of what lies past the limit.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know past the reach of his instruments.

**Stake.** Low to moderate. Portable, and it holds when the instruments are the right ones for the question. Chosen for their names, they are decoration.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-mt-02, cf-mt-04, cf-he-03. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Defamiliarization engine.

**Device cluster.** C (list syntax, negation), E (closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-mt-04 sole custodian

```text
handle: Melville's Tomb | axis: outward | imposed by: a custodian that cannot surrender what it holds
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The knowledge exists and is held by a custodian that cannot give it up.

**Surface.** The close assigns the knowledge, or the image of the lost subject, to one custodian that is not a person, and stops.

**Fold.** Naming a custodian makes the loss possession rather than erasure. The reader learns that what is lost is held somewhere, which is exact and gives no comfort.

**Active site.** The final line.

**Forbidden.** A custodian who could be asked. Consolation from the holding. More than one custodian.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot obtain what the custodian holds.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when the custodian is the actual site of the loss. A custodian chosen for grandeur is a flourish.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-mt-02, cf-mt-03. Cancels with cf-ap-02 and cf-wl-02, since a poem outsources its load-bearing knowledge once.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** E (closure), D (personification).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-jc-02 remains as record

```text
handle: Jewish Cemetery | axis: sideways | imposed by: access to a community only through what it left behind
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker can know a community only through its remains, and reads the remains as the whole of it.

**Surface.** An inventory of what the living left: markers, names, a shut building, a recorded rite. Each item is read for what it says about the people, and none for who keeps it.

**Fold.** Reading remains as record lets the speaker describe a people fully while meeting none of them. The inventory's completeness makes its one omission, the living, easy to miss.

**Active site.** The item that implies a living keeper.

**Forbidden.** A living informant. An item read for its present use. The speaker's entry into the shut building.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the community except through what it left, and he cannot tell remains from what is still in use.

**Stake.** Moderate. Portable to any outsider's visit. It holds when the remains are genuinely all the speaker can reach. Where he could simply ask, it reads as incuriosity.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-jc-03, cf-jc-04, cf-sb-03.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (spatial deixis, witness posture), C (list syntax).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-jc-03 unread evidence

```text
handle: Jewish Cemetery | axis: sideways | imposed by: evidence the speaker records and cannot interpret
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker records a detail that contradicts his conclusion, and he cannot see what it implies.

**Surface.** A single detail, given plainly midway, that the closing statement cannot account for. The poem never returns to it.

**Fold.** The unread detail lets the reader overturn the speaker's conclusion with the speaker's own evidence, so the limit of his vantage is proved from inside the poem rather than asserted from outside it.

**Active site.** The detail. Once, midway, unremarked.

**Forbidden.** Returning to the detail. Marking it with emphasis. A speaker who half-notices. A second such detail, which makes the blindness implausible.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot see what the detail implies. The reader can, from the page alone.

**Stake.** Moderate to high. It depends on a speaker whose blindness is structural rather than careless. If the implication is obvious to anyone paying attention, the speaker reads as a fool and the fold becomes satire.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-jc-02, cf-jc-04, cf-sc-05. Cancels with cf-et-02: disowning access requires a speaker who knows his limits, and unread evidence requires one who cannot see them.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** G (withholding, reader activation), E (asymmetrical closure).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-jc-04 unknowing verdict

```text
handle: Jewish Cemetery | axis: sideways | imposed by: a vantage the closing claim exceeds
rights ceiling: Level 4 available, held at Level 0
```

**Position.** The speaker closes on a general claim his vantage cannot support, and he does not know it cannot.

**Surface.** A closing statement in general terms, confident and sympathetic, about a whole people or a whole future, drawn from a local and partial view.

**Fold.** The verdict's confidence measures the vantage's limit. The larger the claim, the more exactly the reader sees the edge of what the speaker could know.

**Active site.** The closing statement.

**Forbidden.** Hedging the verdict. Irony that signals the speaker knows better. A verdict the vantage could support.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know that his claim exceeds his evidence. The reader can, from the page or from outside it.

**Stake.** High. It can be built honestly only on a real vantage whose limit the writer understands and the speaker does not. A verdict planted in a speaker the writer condescends to is a set-up error, and the reader hears the writer's superiority instead of the speaker's limit.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-jc-02, cf-jc-03. Cancels with cf-sc-05, since two unknowing-register primitives in one trial add nothing to each other, and with cf-he-03, since one must close on a confident claim and the other on a real question.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default, where sympathy launders the verdict.

**Device cluster.** E (closure), G (credibility management).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sm-02 no rival story

```text
handle: Smithville | axis: inward | imposed by: a truth with no narrative form
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The speaker holds a truth that has no story, facing a listener who can receive only stories.

**Surface.** The rival account the speaker might offer is named and found unusable. The speaker then states, as a general law, that what he knows cannot be taught in the form the listener takes.

**Fold.** Declaring his truth untellable concedes the field to the story he disbelieves, and the concession is the evidence that he holds his truth seriously, since he will not dress it up as a story to win.

**Active site.** The general statement of the law.

**Forbidden.** A counter-story. The truth dressed as a story. Contempt for the story that wins.

**Knowledge test.** The listener cannot know the speaker's truth, since no form she can take in carries it.

**Stake.** Moderate to high. It holds where the listener is truly unable to take an argument, as a young child is. With a listener who could, it reads as condescension.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sm-03, cf-cl-02.

**Engines admitted.** Transparent argument as a phase tension.

**Device cluster.** C (declarative flatness, interrogation), G (sayability test).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-sm-03 silent accompaniment

```text
handle: Smithville | axis: inward | imposed by: a rite the speaker cannot join in voice and will not stop
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The speaker takes part in a practice he does not believe, with his voice withheld.

**Surface.** At the close the speaker performs the rite's outward action while withholding its content, stated plainly and once.

**Fold.** Taking part without a voice places the speaker exactly: inside the company, inside the song, outside the belief. The withheld voice is the whole of his dissent, and only the reader can hear it.

**Active site.** The closing action.

**Forbidden.** Explaining the silence. Mockery in the performance. Breaking the silence.

**Knowledge test.** Those beside him cannot know that he withholds. Only the reader can.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds where the rite matters to someone the speaker loves. For a rite with no one he cares about inside it, silent participation is only going along.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-sm-02, cf-wa-03. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** E (closure), G (withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-hl-02 proxy appetite

```text
handle: How to Like It | axis: inward | imposed by: wants the subject cannot own, voiced by another
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The subject's wants can be spoken only by a companion whose wants are simpler than his.

**Surface.** A second voice interrupts at intervals with proposals in one fixed invitational form. The subject never answers in kind, and each proposal is smaller or more bodily than his thought.

**Fold.** The proxy's appetites outline the subject's want by what they are not. The reader reads his longing in the gap between each proposal and his silence.

**Active site.** The first proposal after the subject's longing is established.

**Forbidden.** The subject replying in kind. The proxy offering reflection. A proposal that fits.

**Knowledge test.** The subject cannot know his own want, and the proxy knows only its own.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when the proxy's wants are really available and the subject's are not. Where the subject could simply choose, it is a comic routine.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-hl-03, cf-wl-04.

**Engines admitted.** Complaint loop.

**Device cluster.** B (refrain, anaphora), D (prosopopoeia).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-hl-03 imagined departure

```text
handle: How to Like It | axis: inward | imposed by: a journey the subject will not take
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The subject can reach what he wants only in an imagined journey, which the poem renders in full and which never begins.

**Surface.** An extended itinerary in the future or conditional, detailed and sensory, ending at an arrival. Then a return to the present, with nothing moved.

**Fold.** Rendering the untaken journey in full makes the reader live the departure and then its cancellation. The detail of the imagined road is the measure of the want.

**Active site.** The imagined arrival, undercut at once.

**Forbidden.** Taking the journey. Summarizing it. Irony during the imagining.

**Knowledge test.** The subject cannot know what he would find, since the arrival is imagined. He knows only the road.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when a life really binds him in place. Where he could simply go, it is daydream.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-hl-02, cf-pr-03. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** A (tense shift), C (cumulative sentence), E (turn).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-me-02 involuntary link

```text
handle: Memory | axis: inward | imposed by: memory that joins things without consent
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The speaker cannot choose what his memory joins, and one thing calls up another whether he wants it to or not.

**Surface.** Pairs of unrelated things presented as permanently linked, the link reported and never explained, first in the speaker's life and then in others'.

**Fold.** Reporting links without explaining them makes memory a force acting on the rememberer rather than a faculty he uses. The reader sees him owned by his associations, and the comedy of the first links prepares the reader to accept the law before it is applied where it hurts.

**Active site.** The first link that costs someone more than embarrassment.

**Forbidden.** Explaining a link. A link the speaker controls. A cure.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know or choose why one thing calls up another.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when at least one link carries real cost. With comic links only, it is a list of quirks.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-me-03, cf-me-04, cf-ap-03. See Section D.

**Engines admitted.** Complaint loop, where a link repeats against the rememberer's will.

**Device cluster.** C (list syntax, parataxis), E (serial sequence).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-me-03 withheld answer

```text
handle: Memory | axis: inward | imposed by: an answer the poem holds and will not give
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The poem holds an answer and will not give it.

**Surface.** A question put inside the poem to a third party, the party's assent that the answer is easy, and the poem's end.

**Fold.** Ending at the threshold of the answer transfers it to the reader, who has to supply it from the poem's own logic. The withheld sentence becomes the loudest one in the poem.

**Active site.** The final line.

**Forbidden.** The answer. Any sentence after the threshold. A hint that narrows the answer beyond what the poem's logic already supplies.

**Knowledge test.** The reader cannot know the answer. The poem knows it.

**Stake.** High. It depends on the poem having made the answer inevitable without stating it. Where the answer is arbitrary, the stop is a trick.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-me-02, cf-me-04. Counts as a refusal primitive under the Section D ceiling, so it cancels with cf-sc-02, cf-sb-04, cf-wa-04, and cf-pr-02.

**Engines admitted.** Weak signal.

**Device cluster.** E (asymmetrical closure), G (withholding).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-me-04 borrowed testimony

```text
handle: Memory | axis: inward | imposed by: access to a stranger's suffering only through a published account
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The speaker can know a stranger's suffering only through a report he has read.

**Surface.** The grave material arrives secondhand: a news account, summarized, with the subject's own words quoted inside it. The speaker never meets him.

**Fold.** The secondhand frame keeps the speaker from claiming the stranger's suffering while letting the stranger's words carry it. The speaker's distance becomes the poem's honesty.

**Active site.** The first quoted sentence in the stranger's own voice.

**Forbidden.** The speaker imagining the stranger's interior. Meeting him. Editorializing on the account.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot know the stranger except through the report and its quotations.

**Stake.** Moderate. It holds when the speaker's own material is slight beside the stranger's. Where the report is used to decorate the speaker's feelings, it is appropriation.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-me-02, cf-me-03, cf-jc-02.

**Engines admitted.** Body-cost rule.

**Device cluster.** A (scene/report alternation), D (quotation), G (witness relation, appropriation risk).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-as-02 misfiled register

```text
handle: Assailant | axis: sideways | imposed by: a harm that can enter speech only disguised
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```

**Position.** The speaker can speak of a harm only in a register that belongs to something else.

**Surface.** The harm narrated throughout in the idiom of an unrelated and milder relation, with that idiom's advice, concessions, and proposals intact.

**Fold.** The borrowed idiom fits the harm too well, and its fit becomes the accusation, against the harm and against the idiom that made room for it.

**Active site.** The first proposal of reconciliation.

**Forbidden.** Breaking the register to name the harm. Winking. An idiom that does not fit.

**Knowledge test.** The speaker cannot say the harm in its own terms, and the poem cannot know whether the disguise protects her or erases what happened.

**Stake.** High. It holds only where the harm is real on the page and the borrowed idiom has real purchase on it. With a harm the idiom cannot touch, it is a gag.

**Assembly.** Combines with cf-as-03, cf-as-04, cf-et-02. Cancels with cf-sc-05: one needs a speaker who chooses her register, the other a speaker who cannot see his.

**Engines admitted.** Anti-laundering engine default.

**Device cluster.** D (irony), G (tonal laundering, credibility management).

**Stand-down.** None.

### cf-as-03 address to a stranger

```text
handle: Assailant | axis: sideways | imposed by: an addressee known only by what he did
rights ceiling: in copyright, Level 0, structure only
```
